PREFACE 

During the past century since the organization of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints, no adequate volume on the Old Testament prophets, written by one of our faith has appeared in print. This fact is easy to understand. During the pioneering stage of our sojourn in the West it was not possible to carry on careful and exacting study of the Bible such as had been advocated by Joseph Smith, the prophet-founder of the Church.

Not only so, but our people-like others-found the Old Testament prophets hard to understand. Very few individuals versed in Biblical languages and possessing a sufficient knowledge of Bible manners and customs were available to explain to the people what the prophets were attempting to say-at least in terms that were compatible with the spirit of modern revelation. Add to these difficulties the unattractive form in which the text of the prophets has appeared in many editions of the Authorized Version of the Bible and it is readily apparent why our people have written so little about these great characters. 

In bringing forth this volume on the Minor Prophets 

I have had in mind the following aims: 1. To supply my own and other college students with suitable reading materials concerning these great figures of ancient Israel, and 2. To furnish the average intelligent reader with an interpretation and discussion of the Minor Prophets that shall lead to a finer appreciation of them, their work, and their importance for this generation. 

The translation of the prophets used in this volume has usually been that of the Jewish Publication Society of America, though I have not hesitated to alter it at times

PREFACE 

or to make my own translations.

I am much indebted, of course, to the great scholars of the past and present whose studies have helped me in the preparation of this work. The publications that have been of most use to me are mentioned in the bibliography at the end of the book. The two-volume work of Sir 

George Adam Smith, The Book of the Twelve Prophets, 

(Doubleday, Doran & Company, Inc.), should be especially mentioned. However, I have interpreted the 

Minor Prophets in the light and knowledge of the Gospel of Jesus Christ as revealed in this dispensation. I have not hesitated to use all the resources of modern revelation and modern scholarship available to me in the interest of what I regard to be the truth.

My interest in the Minor Prophets began many years ago. This interest has steadily increased as I have studied and taught them in the original languages. A considerable period of travel, observation, and study in Palestine with my associates of the American School of Oriental Research in Jerusalem gave me unrivalled opportunities to deepen and broaden my understanding of the prophets and of the land that produced them.

The chapter on the prophet Zechariah is perhaps the least satisfactory to myself. Zechariah is by all means the most difficult of the Minor Prophets to interpret and I shall appreciate carefully reasoned suggestions from my readers on the parts which are hard to understand. A number of problems in connection with some of the other 

Minor Prophets need to be solved. I shall also appreciate suggestions with regards to these.

The writer is especially indebted to Elder Joseph Fielding Smith of the Council of the Twelve Apostles of the Church who read the manuscript in detail and gave

CHAPTER I 

THE TWELVE OME FACTS CONCERNING THE BOOK OF THE 

TWELVE. Perhaps many of our readers have TWELVE.-Perhaps never heard of the Book of the Twelve. May we hasten to explain that it is the book which contains the prophecies of the Twelve men we shall consider in this volume. If one were fortunate enough to be able to examine a copy of the scriptures used by the ancient Jews in the time of Christ, it is very probable that one would find it divided into three groups of books some what as follows: (1) The Law, containing the Penta teuch or five books of Moses; (2) the Prophets, beginning with Joshua and ending with Malachi; (3) the 

Writings, beginning with Psalms and ending with Chronicles. The prophets would be found subdivided into two groups of books. The first group would consist of four books, Joshua, Judges, Samuel, and Kings, called by the

Jews the Former Prophets, or, the First Volume of 

Prophets; the second, the Books of Isaiah, Jeremiah,

Ezekiel, and the Twelve, called the Latter Prophets, or the Second Volume of Prophets. The Twelve are better known to us today as the twelve Minor Prophets in distinction from the books known as the Major Prophets. 

The prophets Hosea, Joel, Amos, Obadiah, Jonah, Micah, Nahum, Habakkuk, Zephaniah, Haggai, Zechariah, and

Malachi have had the epithet "Minor" applied to them, simply on the ground of their size when compared with those prophets which precede them, and not with the view of representing them as in any respect inferior in quality or authority. It is much better practice to call the Minor

ΤΟ 

Prophets by their original, canonical, and easily understood title of "The Twelve." 

How "The Twelve" were originally collected and arranged is for us at the present time a matter of obscurity. We do know that, whatever their order, the Twelve have always stood together, even from ancient times. The first mention of the Minor Prophets as the Twelve is by 

Jesus the son of Sirach about the year 180 B. C. Of them he says: 

And may the bones of the Twelve

Prophets revive out of their place, For they comforted Jacob, And redeemed them by assurance of hope.¹ 

Stephen the martyr when quoting Amos 5:25-27 makes a reference to them.

But God turned and gave them (the Israelites) over to worship the host of heaven, as it is written in the Book of the 

Prophets. . . .2

Later Jewish tradition attributed the collection of the sacred books to the men of the Great Synagogue, a body of learned Jewish Scribes said to have been organized by Ezra, and continuing until early in the third century B. C. Nehemiah-in the opinion of many-completed the collection, by adding the inspired writings written in or near his own times. If the tradition be true-which few if any competent authorities admit-it is not unlikely that he was aided by the prophet Malachi who was a contemporary (circa 450 B. C.) of his. 

The order of the Minor Prophets in the Authorized

2 Acts 7:42.

Micah, Joel, Obadiah, and Jonah. 

THE TWELVE AS PROPHETS.-The Minor Prophets are generally considered to be the most obscure and difficult to interpret of all the prophets.

Besides the avoidance of a minute and particular style of description, and the exhibition of the more general aspects of events only, which are justly regarded as essentially characteristic of prophecy, and the exuberance of imagery, which was so admirably calculated to give effect to the oracles delivered by the inspired Seers, but which to us does not possess the vividness and perspicuity which it did to those to whom it was originally exhibited, there are peculiarities attaching more or less to each of the writers, arising either from his matter, or from the manner of its treatment, which presents difficulties of no ordinary magnitude to common readers, and many that are calculated to exercise the ingenuity, and, in no small degree, to perplex the mind of the more experienced interpreter. We are frequently left to guess historical circumstances from what we otherwise know of the features of the times, and sometimes we have no other means of ascertaining their character than what are furnished by the descriptive terms employed in the predictions themselves. Though in such cases general ideas may be collected respecting the persons or things which are presented to view in the text, yet we want the historical commentary which would elucidate and give point to its various particulars. The accounts contained in the books of 

Kings and Chronicles are frequently too brief to furnish us with a key to many of the prophecies which were fulfilled during the period which they embrace; while the pages of profane history only slightly touch, if they touch at all, upon events which the scope and bearing of the predictions determine to periods within the range of subjects professedly treated of by its authors.* 

The Greek translation of the Old Testament. 

E. Henderson, The Book of the Twelve Minor Prophets, and Ed. 

General Preface, vii, viii.

The difficulties of the general reader are often enhanced by a lack of knowledge of Hebrew manners and customs as well as by a lack of knowledge of Hebrew. 

Even the expert Bible scholar is often at a loss to explain a given word, idiom, or allusion that was perfectly clear to the ancients. Ofttimes we are faced with a corrupt text, the difficulties of which the general reader has no adequate conception. On the whole the Hebrew text of the 

Minor Prophets is good, but when, as in the case of Hosea, we meet with some of the most corrupt texts in the Old Testament, the reader must be very tolerant, even with the most experienced interpreter. Some of the prophets use compressed or condensed language, interspersed with metaphors, many of which are derived from sources that accord little with the dictates of occidental taste. The visions of a prophet like Zechariah are difficult, not because of the language, which is simple in character, but because of our lack of knowledge of the meaning of given symbols which represent certain historical scenes and events. Some of the prophets are in the habit of suddenly changing their viewpoint, such as from their own day to the latter days and vice versa. This may puzzle the average reader very much. Only practice in reading the prophets or good instruction can solve this problem. Even the most experienced individual will find himself making an occasional split-hair decision when attempting to solve a problem respecting the period of which a prophet is speaking. Other difficulties will be made apparent to the reader as he studies this volume. But he should not be at all discouraged, for, with the exception of a few very difficult matters, the Minor Prophets can be understood. 

Especially is this true for the sincere Latter-day Saint who has a great advantage in knowing the principles of the

THE TWELVE 

13 Gospel. Modern revelation helps us in solving many of the hitherto difficult problems respecting the Minor Prophets.

Some of the Minor Prophets are among the greatest prophets of all time. It is significant that when the resurrected Savior appeared to the ancient Nephites upon this continent, He made extensive quotations from the Books of Micah and Malachi. When Moroni appeared to the prophet Joseph Smith he quoted and explained passages from Malachi and Joel. Allusions to Zechariah in the 

Doctrine and Covenants show how great and important his prophecies were to the latter day. Add to these Hosea, Amos, and Jonah, so much admired by the Christian world, and there is a very respectable group of great and wonderful prophets among the Twelve. The Minor 

Prophets are not minor.

At this point it may be useful to say something about the nature of a prophet and his functions, the better to understand the Minor Prophets. The word "prophet" originally came from the Greek word "prophetes" and meant "one who speaks for, or on behalf of," another. 

The Hebrew "nabhi," which is translated "prophet," has reference to a man commissioned by God to be a giver or recipient of information with respect to the Unseen World. A prophet might also be a seer with all the powers that the Hebrew words "chozeh" and "ro'eh" connote.

They imply that a seer might gaze on or see the thing of which he speaks or makes known. 

It is evident that many of the Minor Prophets were seers. These men were teachers and counselors of their people. They sternly rebuked those who broke the laws of morality and social righteousness. They counseled justice, kindness, mercy, brotherhood, and humility. They

condemned hypocrisy, pride, undependability, worldli ness, greed, back-sliding, idolatry, oppression, and dishonesty. In most of their work they doubtless had to do with the present needs of their people. They believed that the interest of the Lord was "coextensive with human life and interests" and "they were persuaded that their activity as his representatives should be equally comprehensive. 

Consequently, they felt constrained to interfere in every sphere of human life and to insist on obedience to the divine will in all human relationships." Yet, as seers, they could foresee that if their people were too weak to live the ideals set for them by God, it would be necessary to refine and purge them in the furnace of affliction. A remnant of Israel would thus be left in the dispensation of the fullness of times to be gathered and redeemed. This ray of hope, that of Israel's glorious final destiny, the prophets extended to their people for a double purpose. 

It would cheer the righteous and show them that their works were not in vain. It would warn the wicked of their inevitable destruction if they did not mend their ways. Thus, in the writings of the Minor Prophets, the element of prediction is by no means absent as some have supposed. Only three-at the most, four of the Minor

Prophets fail to mention the glories of Israel in the latter days, and one does not expect it in their writings because of the subjects they were treating. Moroni, as pointed out above, quoted extensively from the Minor Prophets because much of what they said had reference to Israel's destiny and work in our own generation. It is interesting to note that a very competent authority, Principal Alfred

Guillaume, in the Bampton Lectures for 1938 at Oxford 

University, made the following statement: 

'F. C. Eiselen, The Prophetic Books of the Old Testament, I, 108.

THE TWELVE 

15 An epigram which has become widely current asserts that the prophets were 'forthtellers rather than fore-tellers.' Whether this dictum can be sustained is very doubtful. There is no prophet in the Old Testament who was not a foreteller of the future, and though the saying rightly lays stress on the fact that the prophets for the most part spoke in the name of God as men declaring His will and purpose, there can be no doubt whatever that they were feared because it was believed that they knew what was coming. . . .

The prophets usually spoke in general terms of the future, though occasionally they became more specific. 

Thus Malachi speaks of the coming of Elijah for a certain task and Zechariah of the coming of the Messiah to save the Jews when their enemies attack Judah and Jerusalem in the latter days. These matters are quite specific, though their full significance is not clear without the aid of modern revelation.

It is easy to miss the double meaning which certain prophets seem to convey in their writings. Obadiah is a good example of this. He speaks of the coming destruction of Edom, ancient enemy of his people, and then uses her as a symbol of the wicked world in the latter days, whose destruction is sure. The reader is asked to compare verses 2-14 with 15-21. Without careful study one might suppose that the prophet is referring throughout to the destruction of Israel's ancient enemy. In Zephaniah the destruction incident to the coming of the great and terrible day of the Lord-even yet future is used by the prophet to give background for his prediction of imminent punishments to befall his people and those of neighboring countries. If one is not very discerning he 

6 Alfred Guillaume, Prophecy and Divination, p. 111. 

7They were probably not intended originally to have double or hidden meanings. The point is that hitherto, due to faulty interpretation, we have missed part of what the prophets said.

will find himself missing the significance of Zephaniah's reference to the great Day of the Lord.

While the Old Testament is not specific in relation to the matter, Latter-day Saints believe that all or most of the Minor Prophets held the Melchizedek Priesthood. 

We believe that they understood the Gospel of Jesus Christ in much the same light as we do and looked forward to the coming of Christ in the flesh with great eagerness.

That their understanding of the Gospel was very deep is shown in the fact that Malachi, one of their number, predicted the coming of Elijah the prophet with all the keys pertaining to the sealing powers of the Priesthood. 

Other principles of great doctrinal interest were announced by them. The words of an ancient American prophet, Jacob, the brother of Nephi, are apropo at this point. 

Speaking of the holy prophets who came before his day he said:

Behold, they believed in Christ and worshiped the Father in his name, and also we worship the Father in his name. And for this intent we keep the law of Moses, it pointing our souls to him; and for this cause it is sanctified unto us for righteousness, even as it was accounted unto Abraham in the wilderness to be obedient unto the commands of God in offering up his son Isaac, which is a similitude of God and his Only Begotten Son. Wherefore, we search the prophets, and we have many revelations and the spirit of prophecy....9

This passage is a very revealing one for purposes of studying the prophets. It makes clear that in ancient 

8The prophet Joseph Smith said: "Answer to the question, was the

Priesthood of Melchizedek taken away when Moses died? All Priesthood is

THE TWELVE 

17 Israel the prophets kept the Law of Moses, so far as its outward ordinances were concerned, but in other respects observed the principles of the Gospel. They taught their relatively small number of followers to do likewise. The high type of religion taught by the Minor Prophets is thus explained. They denounced certain ritualistic observances only when performed unworthily, but plainly implied that the Law of Moses was not sufficient to bring salvation to any man.

THE MINOR PROPHETS IN THE "INSPIRED” RE- VISION OF THE BIBLE.-Between June, 1830 and July, 

1833, the prophet Joseph Smith revised the Bible by the spirit of revelation. Study has shown that he did not completely finish the task, which he probably would have done had not he been martyred in the year 1844.10 In the New Testament and in a few Old Testament books he made many revisions, though even in these he did not complete his work. The Minor Prophets seem to have been revised only very imperfectly. This is unfortunate, for the readings of the prophet usually show keen penetration and are very useful in clearing up matters previously obscure. In Hosea, perhaps the most corrupt book in point of text in the Old Testament, the prophet seems to have corrected only three verses, 6:1, 10; 11:8. The changes in 6:1, 10 are inconsequential. In 11:8 the prophet reads: "My heart is turned toward thee, and my mercies are extended to gather thee," instead of the 

Authorized Version reading, "Mine heart is turned within me, my repentings are kindled together." In Joel two 

10See Sidney B. Sperry and Merrill Y. Van Wagoner, "The Inspired 

Revision of the Bible," Imp. Era, XLIII, 206, 270, 336, 408, 472, 536. (April- 

September, 1940.)

verses are corrected: 2:13, 14. These read as follows: 

And rend your hearts, and not your garments, and repent, and turn unto the Lord your God; for He is gracious and merciful, slow to anger, and of great kindness, and he will turn away the evil from you.

Therefore, repent, and who knoweth but he will return and leave a blessing behind him; that you may offer a meat offering, and a drink offering, unto the Lord your God?

The corresponding text of the Authorized Version follows: 

And rend your heart, and not your garments, and turn unto the Lord your God: for he is gracious and merciful, slow to anger, and of great kindness, and repenteth him of the evil. 

Who knoweth if he will return and repent, and leave a blessing behind him; even a meat offering and a drink offering unto the Lord your God. 

The text of Amos is corrected in eight verses: 3:6, 7; 

4:3, 5, 6; 5:3; 6:10; 7:3. The changes in some of thes are very interesting. We offer 3:6, 7 as a sample: 

Shall a trumpet be blown in the city, and the people not be afraid? Shall there be evil in a city, and the Lord hath not known it?

Surely the Lord God will do nothing until he revealeth the secret unto his servants the prophets.

The Authorized Version renders: 

Shall a trumpet be blown in the city, and the people not be afraid? Shall there be evil in a city, and the Lord hath not done it?

Surely the Lord God will do nothing, but he revealeth his secret unto his servants the prophets.

The prophet made no changes in Obadiah, but in Jonah he revised 3:9, 10 to read: 

Who can tell, if we will repent, and turn unto God, but he will turn away from us his fierce anger, that we perish not?

THE TWELVE 

19 And God saw their works that they turned from their evil way and repented; and God turned away the evil that he had said he would bring upon them.

No changes are made in Micah, though its text is often corrupt. In Nahum 3:16 an inconsequential change is made. Habakkuk is not revised and the same is true of Zephaniah. Two unimportant changes are made in the text of Haggai, but in Zechariah five verses are revised: 4:10; 5:11; 6:5; 8:7, 13. Some of the revisions in Zechariah are simple ones, but are of importance in the interpretation of the prophecy. Malachi was not changed in the prophet's revision.

Looking at it from the point of view of the student of the text of the Minor Prophets, it is apparent that 

Joseph Smith never completed his revision of them, for many textual corruptions well known to scholars have never been touched.

THE USE OF THE MINOR PROPHETS IN THE BOOK OF MORMON AND THE DOCTRINE AND COVENANTS.- 

The Book of Mormon makes few allusions to the Minor Prophets. No allusions are made to those who lived after 

600 B. C. with the exception of Malachi, two chapters of which were quoted to the Nephites by the risen Savior. I 

Nephi 22:15 makes an apparent allusion to Mal. 4:1, which needs to be explained since the prophet Nephi lived nearly one hundred and fifty years before Malachi prophesied. The explanation occurring to the writer is that 

Malachi made an allusion to a much earlier scripture, which was also known to Nephi through the Brass Plates. ¹¹ Hence, Malachi's reference to the wicked being burned as stubble is possibly not original with him. The case of the appar- 11 11Or, perhaps, the same revelation was given to both prophets who expressed the substance of it in somewhat comparable terms.

ent allusions of 2 Nephi 26:9 and Ether 9:22 to Mal. 

4:2 is most peculiar, but may be explained on somewhat similar principles. The reader will find a footnote that deals in part with this case in the chapter on Malachi. 

2 Nephi 28:25 may be an allusion to Amos 6:1. Other noteworthy quotations or allusions to the Minor Prophets are as follows: 3 Nephi 20:16-19 cf. Micah 4:12, 13; 

5:8-15; 3 Nephi 21:12-21 cf. Micah 5:8-15; 3 Nephi 

24, 25 cf. Mal. 3, 4. There may be other allusions that have slipped the eye of the writer, but it is believed they will be found very few in number.

Most of the allusions to the Minor Prophets and quotations from the same found in the Doctrine and Covenants are confined to the Books of Malachi, Joel, and Zechariah. One doubtful allusion may be to Haggai. The fact that strong allusions to Zechariah are made may point to the fact that Moroni also quoted from that prophet, as he did from Malachi and Joel, when he visited Joseph 

Smith on the evening of September 21, 1823. Following is the list of allusions and quotations referred to. It cannot claim to be exhaustive, but for most practical purposes will be found to be nearly so: D. & C. 2; 27:9; 98:16, 17; 110:13-16; 128:17 cf. Malachi 4:5, 6; D. & C. 60:4 cf. Mal. 3:17; D. & C. 64:23, 24 cf. Mal. 4:1; D. & C. 

128:24 cf. Mal. 3:1-3; D. & C. 133:64 cf. Mal. 4:1;

21 imply that the latter is quoting from or alluding to the texts in question.

Some students of the Doctrine and Covenants have been disturbed over the fact that its revelations contain many allusions to the Bible as well as some quotations from it. But why should this fact disturb one? Prophets in the Bible quote other prophets in their revelations or make frequent allusions to them. Furthermore, if an ancient prophet makes some statement that applies to the latter day, why should not the prophet Joseph Smith, through the spirit of revelation, reinforce that truth at the proper place and time by making reference to it?

CHAPTER II HOSEA-PROPHET OF LOVE ACTS AND SUPPOSITIONS CONCERNING THE PRO- 

PHET.-The superscription of his book informs us that Hosea was the son of Beeri. Unfortunately we know nothing about the latter. The Hebrew name of the prophet, Hoshea, signifies "help," "deliverance," and "salvation," and is derived from the same root as the names of Joshua and Jesus. By reason of numerous allusions in the prophecy to the Northern Kingdom, it is commonly supposed by commentators that Hosea was a native of that commonwealth. The superscription further informs us that Hosea was a prophet "in the days of Uzziah, Jotham, Ahaz, and Hezekiah, kings of Judah, and in the days of Jeroboam the son of Joash, king of 

Israel." Jeroboam II, king of Israel, reigned from 788

B. C. until 747 B. C.¹ We may not be far from the truth, therefore, if we date Hosea's ministry from about 

755 B. C. to 725 B. C.2 He was, then, a contemporary of three other great prophets, Isaiah, Amos, and Micah. 

Under what conditions did Hosea live as a youth? 

How was he raised? What were the circumstances under which God called him to the ministry? When did he receive the Holy Melchizedek Priesthood? All these questions and a host of others that we should like answered must be passed by for a lack of specific information. All we know about Hosea and his ministry is contained in the 

1These dates follow the chronology given in T. H. Robinson, A History of Israel, I, 463f. Dates of the reigns of the kings of Israel and Judah are often only approximate or conjectural.

Many authorities believe that his ministry did not extend beyond 735 B. C. A consideration of this point is beyond the purview of this book.

short fourteen chapters of the book that goes under his name. But it is perfectly apparent to anyone that a prophet of God does not function in a social vacuum. 

Hosea had much work to do, many sermons to preach, and found it necessary to provide leadership for a group of disciples who were true followers of the Hebrew Jehovah. Prophets of the Lord are much concerned about people. It is not at all unlikely that the disciples presided over by Hosea fellowshipped with other folk having similar ideals in various centers of population in Israel and Judah. These other folk were in turn under the leadership of prophets like Isaiah, Amos, Micah, or other Godfearing men, at present unknown to us. Let us call the little bands of disciples the Hebrew Church of God. It is not unreasonable to suppose that leaders like Hosea, Micah,

Isaiah, Amos, and others, conferred with one another respecting the common problems found in each of their branches of the Church. Not unlike the situation in our own day, the Church had to contend with problems that arose because of the beliefs and practices of other churches or cults among the Hebrews. Witness the action of

Amaziah, the hireling or professional priest of Bethel, in attempting to prevent the prophet Amos from giving his message to Israel. Amaziah, in this instance, seems to have acted as a professional agent of a state religion. that was all but compatible with the prophetic spirit of men like Amos, Hosea, Isaiah, and Micah.

We are justified in believing that the prophets were social beings who, more especially after their calls to the ministry, moved about and influenced many people. It is not reasonable to believe that a prophet like Hosea served only as a messenger of warning and admonition at given. 

Amos 7:10-17.

intervals over a period of years and had no further duties other than to make a living. And we can take it for granted that only a very few of Hosea's prophecies have been preserved in the book that has come down to us. 

All answers to questions concerning Hosea's domestic life hinge on the interpretation given to chapters 1-3. 

The latter have been very diversely interpreted by able commentators and for the moment we shall pass by the problem of the prophet's domestic status. 

Of Hosea's personal characteristics we frankly know little. Students of his prophecy may differ more or less in their estimate of him. The following by Professor G. 

L. Robinson is submitted for its interest:

He was gentle, pensive, and inclined to melancholy, but frank, affectionate, and full of domestic feeling. He was the Jeremiah of the Northern Kingdom, and, like him, was little less than a martyr, prefiguring Christ. Both were ardent patriots; possessed of finely sensitive religious natures. Jeremiah was the more studied and self-conscious in his grief; Hosea, the more artless and passionate. Jeremiah was more of a theologian; Hosea, more of a poet.¹

THE PROPHET'S TIMES.-In the days of Hosea the 

Northern Kingdom had been lifted by Jeroboam II to a commanding position of power and wealth. Israel's ancient boundaries had been restored and her leaders and chief men were haughty and proud. The Southern Kingdom of Judah, too, had been revived under King Uzziah in a really marvelous way according to the account in 

II Chron. 26:6-15. Both kingdoms prospered greatly, especially from the booty taken in conquest and the revival of trade. The rich build “summer houses” and “winter 

4G. L. Robinson, The Twelve Minor Prophets, pp. 15 f.

houses,' "houses of ivory," and "houses of ebony."5

Their houses were furnished with costly articles of furniture, and for their own personal adornment they insisted on gorgeous robes and quantities of jewelry. They indulged in the luxuries of the table, taking for themselves the best of the veal and lamb that the country afforded. 

The wives of men in high places set the high standards of comfort and social life and insisted on drinking wine, regardless of the cost. The prophet Amos called them "cows."6

These debauched women oppressed the poor, crushed the needy, and in numerous ways seriously weakened the moral fiber of their people. 

Social and economic changes were widespread. The small landowner disappeared. He was supplanted by the wealthy landlord who created large estates out of little farms. Widows and orphans had little or no protection.

Sons were often mortgaged and finally lost as slaves when the mortgage could not be cleared. Such practices were known as early as the days of Elisha." Tenants on large estates paid rents that were ruinous. Often they became slaves as a result of this practice. Exactions of grain were made upon the poor by the upper crust of society. Seething want and corruption were everywhere apparent. There was cheating in the market places and perjury in the law courts, and politicians looked to their own interests rather than to the nation's they were supposed to serve. On the one hand, we find selfish and arrogant luxury facing ragged and loathsome squalor on the other. These conditions meant that Israel was rotten to the core and ripe for destruction. 

5See Amos 3:15.

A very simple change in the Hebrew gives the reading "ebony" which makes good sense. 6 Amos 4:1. 

7II Kings 4:1-7.

Although so greatly needed, the religion of the time had little hope and direction to offer the great masses of the people. So far as the official cults were concerned, religion in the larger centers such as Jerusalem, Gilgal, Bethel, and Beersheba, had made little change for the better in over a hundred years-from the days of Ahab to Jeroboam II. Jehovah, to be sure, was worshipped, but as an old Canaanite Baal. Note the following words used by Hosea: 

And I will visit upon her the days of the Baals, And it shall be at that day, saith the Lord, That thou shalt call Me "My husband"; 

And shalt call Me no more "My Baal." 

For I will take away the names of the 

Baals out of her mouth.

Most of the people of the time thought that they were engaged in the true worship of Jehovah, but Hosea makes plain that their worship was in reality no more than a revival of an old Baal cult. To be sure, men were outwardly more devout and more scrupulous in religious observances than ever before. The courts of the temple and other religious shrines were filled with crowds; altars smoked with sacrifices; tithes were paid exactly when due; the Sabbath and other impressive occasions were observed with meticulous care. But all of this ceremonial swank failed to touch Israel's real evils. The religion of her leaders had little or no concern with social morality; in fact, religion not merely condoned many of the evils of which we have spoken, but actually enjoined them. Such 

8Hosea 2:13, 16, 17.

is true of immorality, and ritual fornication was by no means unknown. Says Professor Theodore H. Robinson:

So far from giving help and guidance to the people in the great moral and social crisis of her history, the established religion was much more likely to have thrown all its weight into the other scale, and to have fostered those very tendencies which threatened the life of the nation.⁹ 

But with the moral decay of Israel the thing that seems to have impressed and saddened Hosea most was the state of family life. It had become dissolute, and, accordingly, the prophet lays upon it his heaviest indictment. The 

Hebrew root zanah, translated variously as "whoredom," "harlotry," "adultery"-words so distasteful to us—is used by the prophet over twenty times to express his opinion of the situation.

Such were the times in which Hosea prophesied. A knowledge of them will help us substantially in understanding the prophet's problem and message.

SOME OBSERVATIONS REGARDING THE BOOK.—The 

Hebrew text of Hosea is more corrupt than that of any other Old Testament book. It has suffered seriously in the process of transmission, and certain parts of it defy successful translation. Some students think that there is scarcely a verse which one can be certain is in the form in which it left the pen of the original writer. The style of the book is broken, and to a remarkable degree the text is incapable of logical division. This is especially true in chapters 4-14. 

Hosea may be divided into two sections of very unequal length. The first section, chapters 1-3, is a fairly compact unit, containing much narrative which 

9T. H. Robinson, op. cit., I, 368.

purports to tell the domestic life of the prophet. Whether regarded as symbolic action or otherwise in the narrative, God commands the prophet to "take a wife of harlotry" for the purpose of begetting children whose significant names were to set forth to Israel the disastrous fruits of her spiritual whoredom.

In the second section, chapters 4-14, we find, as Sir 

George Adam Smith has said, "a stream of addresses and reflections, appeals, upbraidings, sarcasms, recollections of earlier history, denunciations and promises, which, with little logical connection and almost no pauses or periods, start impulsively from each other, and for a large part are expressed in elliptic and ejaculatory phrases."'10

Israel's spiritual adultery, which the prophet has described in the first section, is more elaborately dealt with here. He tells of God's controversy with His people, their moral decay, and the punishment that must inevitably follow. Judah is also referred to occasionally. Finally, he appends a solemn appeal to return to Jehovah and promises that the Lord will show compassion to the penitent and heal their apostasy.

Hosea abounds in sentences of irregular construction. 

Novel forms of syntax, pecular terms of expression, and unusual formations of words and phrases are also to be noted. Most of the book is rhythmical, and abounds in highly figurative and metaphorical language. Similes and metaphors are frequently so intermixed that it is difficult to discover their exact bearing and force. The diction of the prophet is concise and laconic; number and gender are often neglected; the unexpected change of person is of frequent occurrence. Use of particles is infrequent as compared with other prophets, which adds not a little to the 

10G. A. Smith, The Book of the Twelve Minor Prophets (New and 

Revised Edition), I, 219.

69 difficulty of interpretation. Often Hosea is lively, energetic, and sublime. From the viewpoint of language, he is the most obscure and difficult to understand of all the prophets.

We may now profitably turn to the teaching of the book.

THE STORY OF HOSEA'S STRANGE MARRIAGE.-In the first section of the prophecy, chapters 1-3, we find the strange story of Hosea's marriage to a profligate woman. 

A person reading the text for the first time may be considerably agitated when he finds that the marriage is not only condoned, but is actually commanded by the Lord. 

Go, take unto thee a wife of harlotry and children of harlotry; for the land doth commit great harlotry, departing from the Lord.11

Accordingly, Hosea takes to wife a woman by the name of Gomer, who bears to him two sons and a daughter. These children were given names that symbolized the 

Lord's intention of taking action against Israel because of her evil and apostate condition. The first son was called 

Jezreel, "Whom God Sows." 

For yet a little while, And I will visit the blood of Jezreel 

Upon the house of Jehu, And will cause to cease the kingdom of the house of Israel.¹ 12 The first daughter was named Lo-ruhamah, “That hath not obtained compassion." 

For I will no more

Have compassion upon the house of Israel, That I should in any wise pardon them.¹ 13 11 Hosea 1:2. 

12Hosea 1:4. 

13Hosea 1:6.

The third and last child, a son, was given the name of Lo-ammi, "Not my people." 

For ye are not My people, And I will not be yours.14

What a strange situation for a prophet to find himself in married to such a wife and having children with such ominous and undesirable names. Is there any wonder that the story of Hosea's marriage has had so many different interpretations placed upon it? How are we to construe the story? Is it to be interpreted literally or is it to be taken as an allegory or parable? Views of capable commentators have been very diverse, but we may for convenience classify them under four heads. 

1. The story is an allegory or parable symbolizing the relation existing between Jehovah and His people, Israel. 

2. The story is assumed to be a literal account of actual facts. Many ancient interpreters took this position and several modern critics have presented the view with considerable force and ingenuity. 

3. The modified literal theory assumes that Gomer, Hosea's wife, was not a common harlot, but rather a Baal worshipper who was, therefore, guilty of spiritual harlotry. She may even have been a priestess of the cult, who had sacrificed her physical as well as her spiritual chastity by reason of her fanatic devotion to it. Hosea could not, therefore, bring charges against her or have her put to death, because her position was considered honorable or sacred at the time. By ingenious interpretation or emendation of the text of Hosea 3:2, those who hold to this 

14Hosea 1:9.

theory claim that what Hosea did was to pay Gomer's vow or debt to the shrine to which she resorted, and then bring her home and isolate her. 

4. Many, if not most, modern expositors hold the view that Gomer was a pure woman when Hosea married her, and continued so until after the birth of her first child.

Which of the above views is the correct one is, of course, difficult at the present time to say.15 Very probably the truth is that none of them is completely correct as expounded to date. Nor ought we to be surprised at this. Long ago, Nephi, a Book of Mormon prophet, who came from Jerusalem about 600 B. C., said:

I know that the Jews do understand the things of the prophets, and there is none other people that understand the things which were spoken unto the Jews like unto them, save it be that they are taught after the manner of the things of the Jews.16

And we are forced to admit that many allusions and figures of speech in the prophets are not yet completely understood.

The writer takes the following view of Hosea's marriage or marriages¹7 in the first three chapters of the prophecy: The Lord's call to Hosea to take a harlotrous woman to wife represents the prophet's call to the ministry -a ministry to an apostate and covenant-breaking people. 

The evil children of this apparent union represent the coming of the judgments of the Lord upon Israel, warning of which was to be carried to the people by the prophet. The 

15 For a more detailed consideration of these views, see Smith, op. cit., 246ff. 

1611 Nephi 25:5. 

17 See Hosea 3:1.

figure of the harlotrous wife and children would, I believe, be readily understood at the time by the Hebrew people without reflecting on Hosea's own wife, or, if he was unmarried, on himself.

For there is an essential difference between a merely symbolical representation, and the actual performance of anything. 

The instruction given to a prophet to set forth a sin in a symbolical form, for the purpose of impressing upon the hearts of the people its abominable character, and the punishment it deserved, is not at variance with the holiness of God; whereas the command to commit a sin would be. God, as the Holy One, cannot abolish the laws of morality, or command anything actually immoral, without contradicting Himself, or denying His own nature.18

This symbolic representation of God's call to Hosea would be a most forceful and effective means of calling Israel's attention to her carnal state. I cannot believe the marriage to be a literal one, for, as those who have taken it as an allegory or parable have always pointed out, to do so would be imputing to God a command inconsistent with His holy character. Furthermore, for Hosea to marry a woman with a questionable past would make it impossible for him to preach to his people and expose their sexual immoralities. They could point the finger of scorn at him and say, "You are as guilty as we are; don't preach to us." Two other considerations lead me to believe the marriage was symbolical. Seven years at least would have passed after Hosea's marriage before the birth of the third child occurred. Most Oriental women let at least two years pass before weaning a child. Seven years is a long 

18C. F. Keil and F. Delitzsch, Biblical Commentary on the Old Testament 

(The Twelve Minor Prophets), I, 35, note. Occasionally it would appear that for a higher purpose the Lord can transcend the moral order which he has established to guide man. Note His command to Abraham to slay 

Isaac and also the requirement made of Nephi to slay Laban.

time to set forth relatively simple warnings represented by the names of the children. Even some years would have to pass before any great number of people in the nation would pay any attention to the three offspring. 

Finally, in chapter 3:1-3, we have another account of a marriage Hosea contracts with an unchaste woman. There are those who hold that this is not in reality another woman, that the scripture has reference to the final redemption of Gomer, the original wife mentioned in chapter 

1. To this explanation I cannot accede. The language seems plain and unequivocal. It refers to a woman, whereas if it had referred to Gomer more specific language could have been expected. 

And the Lord said unto me: 'Go yet, love a woman beloved of her friend and an adulteress, even as the Lord loveth the children of Israel, though they turn unto other gods, and love cakes of raisins.' So I bought her to me for fifteen pieces of silver and a homer of barley, and a half homer of barley; and I said unto her: 'Thou shalt sit solitary for me many days; thou shalt not play the harlot, and thou shalt not be any man's wife; nor will I be thine.'

Surely no one could reasonably expect the Lord to command two such literal marriages for a prophet. I take it that the very literal portrayal of these marriages was a representation to the prophet of the real and very grim nature of the call he had to warn Israel of her misdeeds. 

The fourth view given above, which is held by many modern expositors, claims too much. It avoids imputing to God a command requiring Hosea to marry a profligate woman by assuming that Gomer was a pure woman until after the birth of her first child. The text, however, gives no inkling of this; it simply says, "Go, take unto thee a wife of harlotry and children of harlotry." Neither can

I see any good reason for denying to Hosea the last two children mentioned in chapter 1. The text implicitly assumes that all are his. Anything beyond this puts on the story a strain that it will not bear. 

But whether the writer's interpretation of Hosea's marriages be accepted or some other, the religious significance of chapters 1-3 is quite clear. Hosea's wives represent 

Israel, the disloyal and harlotrous consort of Jehovah. 

The latter stipulates that unless Israel puts aside her harlotries and reforms she will meet with stern action. For her gross sins she will be checked and punished and thus learn in the crucible of bitter experience that her husband means more to her than she at first supposed. 

Therefore, behold, I will hedge up thy way with thorns, And I will make a wall against her, That she shall not find her paths. 

And she shall run after her lovers, but she shall not overtake them, And she shall seek them, but shall not find them; 

Then shall she say: "I will go and return to my first husband; 

For then was it better with me than now."19 

Jehovah's steadfast affection for Israel despite her waywardness through the years is shown in His attempts to woo her again. 

Therefore, behold, I will allure her, And bring her into the wilderness, And speak tenderly to her.20 

His tender and compassionate wooing will result in 

Israel's eventual return to the fold. No longer will she indulge in the vicious Canaanite immoralities mentioned so frequently in chapter 2.

And shalt call Me no more "My Baal.”

THE LORD'S CONTROVERSY WITH ISRAEL.-In the second section of Hosea's prophecy, chapters 4-14, we shall take notice of a number of teachings that are worthy of special attention. One of the finest of these is that the Lord objects to the lack of knowledge-religious knowledgeamong His people. This lack of knowledge is destroying them. 

Hear the word of the Lord, ye children of Israel!

For the Lord hath a controversy with the inhabitants of the land, Because there is no truth, nor mercy, Nor knowledge of God in the land.

Swearing and lying, and killing, and stealing, and committing adultery! 

They break all bounds, and blood toucheth blood.24 

Notice truth, mercy, knowledge. What a trilogy!

Truth and love are mutually conditions, the one of the other. "Truth cannot be sustained without mercy; and mercy without truth makes men negligent; so that the one ought to be mingled with the other" (Jerome). They both have their roots in the knowledge of God, of which they are the fruit (Jer. 22:16; Isa. 

11:9); for the knowledge of God is not merely "an acquaintance with His nature and will" (Hitzig), but knowledge of the love, faithfulness, and compassion of God, resting upon the experience of the heart. Such knowledge not only produces fear of God, but also love and truthfulness towards brethren. Where this is wanting, injustice gains the upper hand.25

The prophet continues in similar vein, but accuses the priests and professional prophets in particular. 24 Hosea 4:1, 2. 

25Keil and Delitsch, op. cit., 74ff.

Yet let no man strive, neither let any man reprove;

For thy people are as they that strive with the priest. 

Therefore shalt thou stumble in the day, And the prophet also shall stumble with thee in the night; 

And I will destroy thy mother.

My people are destroyed for lack of knowledge; 

Because thou has rejected knowledge, I will also reject thee, that thou shalt be no priest to Me; 

Seeing thou hast forgotten the law of thy God, I also will forget thy children.26

The common people, guilty as they are, are not so guilty or responsible as their religious counselors who live upon their (the people's) fines and sin-offerings. Priests and prophets alike have given themselves over to formal ritual and have forgotten that they have an intellectual and moral responsibility to the nation. Let us never forget that Hosea and the other true prophets of Israel always kept their eyes open to the fact that priesthood is an intellectual, as well as a moral, trust Hosea adds another statement that is well worth quoting. 

Harlotry, wine, and new wine take away the heart.27 

The word "heart" in Hebrew is often equivalent to our English words "brains" or "understanding." So again

Hosea is administering an intellectual rebuke. He means here that sexual immorality and strong drink (anciently they went hand in hand as now) weaken mental power and produce "a people stupid and falling to ruin."'28

A WARNING AGAINST ENTANGLING ALLIANCES.— 

We live at a time when the drums of war cause many people in our country to debate whether or not we ought 26Hosea 4:4-6. The word mother in this passage has reference to the whole nation and kingdom as in chapter 2:2. 

27Hosea 4:11. 

28 Hosea 4:14.

to make alliances with other countries in self defense. 

During Hosea's ministry there occurred conspiracies and other internal disturbances that seriously weakened Israel (II Kings 15). In desperation the people alternately sought aid from Assyria and Egypt, paying tribute to both, with the result that they lost their independence and national autonomy, being forced to accept vassalage to Assyria. Hosea warned the nation of its folly in seeking alliances with foreign nations. Political alliances would not remedy the real cause of their trouble-moral disease and rebellion against God. Hosea doubtless believed that 

God would protect His own if they but trusted in Him.

And Ephraim is become like a silly dove, without understanding; 

They call unto Egypt, they go to Assyria.29

Hosea wanted his people to avoid making covenants with nations whose sole reliance was on force. Let the big nations fight their own wars. Little nations that elected to mix up with them were sure to be worsted. The big nations, furthermore, had religious practices that were totally and utterly opposite to prophetic ideals. Their immoralities, added to those already prevalent in Israel would, in time, wreck the nation. So Israel would spread the net of destruction over herself. Instead of courting God's love and protection her courting of the nations would only put her in a trap-and it did.

Even as they go, I will spread My net upon them; 

I will bring them down as the fowls of the heaven; 

I will chastise them, as their congregation hath been made to hear. 

Woe unto them! for they have strayed from Me; 

Destruction unto them! for they have transgressed against Me.30 

29 Hosea 7:11. 

30Hosea 7:12, 13.

GOD'S LOVE FOR HIS PEOPLE.-We have noted some of the transgressions that Hosea believed would bring about the fall of Israel. Professor George L. Robinson traces in Hosea the successive steps in Israel's national downfall as follows: 1. Lack of knowledge, 2. Pride, 3. Instability, 4. Worldliness, 5. Corruption, 6. Backsliding, 7. Idolatry. 31 It is apparent that inward corruption of a nation is more dangerous to its existence than external enemies. France in our own time has found out this bitter lesson taught so long ago by the prophets. In presenting God's grim message to His people, Hosea's heart must have been heavy. But despite the corruption of that generation, God gives Hosea a ray of hope for a future time. His love for His people is such that He cannot give them up completely. 

How shall I give thee up, Ephraim? 

How shall I surrender thee, Israel? 

How shall I make thee as Admah? 

How shall I set thee as Zeboim? 

My heart is turned within Me, My compassions are kindled together. 

I will not execute the fierceness of Mine anger, I will not return to destroy Ephraim; 

For I am God, and not man, The Holy One in the midst of thee, And I will not come in fury.32

The above is a great scripture, perhaps the greatest in Hosea. It breathes the spirit of the Man of Galilee whose mercy and love for His brethren transcend all understanding. The passage does not mean that Israel shall escape the due consequences of all her sins. But it does foreshadow the long arm of Jehovah's love reaching for 

31 Robinson, op. cit., pp. 23-26. 

32Hosea 11:8, 9.

her in the day of redemption—the latter days—and rescuing the little righteous remnant of which Isaiah and other prophets also speak.

Finally, Hosea is shown, as it were, Israel's redemption, and in a beautiful passage the Lord says: 

I will be as the dew unto Israel; 

He shall blossom as the lily, And cast forth his roots as Lebanon. 

His branches shall spread, And his beauty shall be as the olive-tree, And his fragrance as Lebanon.

They that dwell under his shadow shall again 

Make grain to grow, And shall blossom as the vine; 

The scent thereof shall be as the wine of Lebanon.

CHAPTER III
