JOEL-PROPHET OF THE GREAT AND TERRIBLE DAY OF THE LORD ITTLE KNOWN OF THE PROPHET.-Little is known of Joel beyond the scanty information that may be gathered from the record that goes under his name. The superscription tells us simply that he was the son of Pethuel. The Hebrew Yo'el, seems to mean "Jehovah is God," and we may be justified in assuming that the prophet's parents were devotees of the true Hebrew religion. The name must have been a rather common one among the ancient Hebrews, for at least a dozen other men in the Old Testament bore it. The name of Joel's father, Pethuel, is thought to mean "Persuaded of God."

It is commonly supposed that Joel was a native of Judah. 

This is inferred from references he makes to Zion, Judah, and Jerusalem, the children of Zion, and to certain religious practices. Such evidence is by no means decisive, but is the only guide scholars can find at hand. As to the circumstances surrounding Joel's call to the ministry, unfortunately nothing has been recorded. 

THE DATE OF THE BOOK.-Few books in the Old 

Testament have been assigned to as many different dates as has the Book of Joel. It has been dated from before 

900 B. C. to 400 B. C., and some parts of it still later. 

Most writers are agreed that the prophecy should be dated either very early or very late. To the average reader it will probably not be found very profitable or stimulating to enter upon the pro's and con's of the arguments for either dating. Most scholars at the present time lean toward a late date of about 400 B. C. The author, however,

is inclined to favor a date of 800 B. C. or a little earlier. 

He feels that the reasons of those favoring a late date are quite as indecisive as they say the premises are for an early date. And so it goes. But, be it said that the date of Joel will have little or no bearing upon our exposition of the prophecy. The message of Joel is important for Latterday Saints irrespective of the date.

THE CONTENTS AND STYLE OF THE PROPHECY.- 

On the surface, at least, Joel appears to deal with the following: 1. A lamentation over the devastation of the land by locusts, fire, and drought. 2. The destruction of the army of locusts, and a renewal of spiritual and material blessings. 3. The outpouring of God's spirit upon all flesh. 4. Judgment upon the nations, and the eternal deliverance and glorification of God's people.

Presently we shall interpret this material in some detail. Something should first be said, however, about the style of the prophecy. The Hebrew text of Joelunlike Hosea in this respect-is quite sound and free from extraneous influences. The prophet is fluent and clear, but uses peculiar constructions, and gives uncommon meanings to common words. Most of the prophecy is in poetry, the superscription and a few verses in chapter 3 being the only exceptions. Many years ago a noted scholar wrote concerning the style of Joel in the followlowing words:

In point of style Joel stands pre-eminent among the Hebrew prophets. He not only possesses a singular degree of purity, but is distinguished by his smoothness and fluency; the animated and rapid character of his rhythmus; the perfect regularity of his parallelisms; and the degree of roundness which he gives to his sentences. He has no abrupt transitions, is everywhere connected, and finishes whatever he takes up. In description he is graphic and perspicuous; in arrangement lucid; in imagery original, co-

pious and varied. In the judgment of Knobel, he most resembles 

Amos in regularity, Nahum in animation, and in both respects 

Habakkuk; but is surpassed by none of them. That what we now possess is all he ever wrote, is in the highest degree improbable: on the contrary, we should conclude from the cultivated character of his language, that he had been accustomed to composition long before he penned these discourses. Whatever degree of obscurity attaches to his book, is attributable to our ignorance of the subjects of which it treats, not to the language which he employs.¹ 

In certain respects Dr. Henderson is seconded by 

Professor Orelli who says:

Also the simple, lofty, and classically finished language testifies to high antiquity. In point of form the book is one of the most perfect pieces of the Old Testament; and this perfection rests, not on technical diligence, but on genuine poetic elevation, which spontaneously communicates itself to the style. Prophecy and poetry are here inseparably blended.2

The above beautiful tributes to Joel's style give evidence of the respect in which the prophet's writings have been held. We may now deal profitably with the vital questions relative to the interpretation of the prophecy. 

PAST INTERPRETATIONS OF THE PROPHECY.-As indicated above, Joel speaks of three great calamities-a plague of locusts, fire, and drought-that foreshadow the 

Day of the Lord. Chapter 1:2-20 gives a really terrifying and vivid description of the woes to come upon mankind and beasts. Then follows in chapter 2:1-17 another vivid description of the calamity and an urgent exhortation to repentance. A beautiful picture of the blessings 

God will eventually give to His people is then given in verses 18-27. 

1E. Henderson, The Book of the Twelve Minor Prophets, 2nd Ed., pp. 90f. 

2C. Von Orelli, The Twelve Minor Prophets, p. 77.

Now the question naturally arises when we begin to interpret the book, Does Joel describe an actual plague of locusts in his own day or were they to come in the future? Are the locusts real or do they represent in symbolic form future disasters upon mankind? Interpreters of the book have generally classified it under the three following categories: 1. Description, 2. Allegory, 3. Apoc alypse (Revelation). Let us consider each of these briefly. 

We may well quote Sir George Adam Smith who gives a brief statement in favor of the first of these. 

A large majority of critics hold that Joel starts, like other prophets, from the facts of experience. His locusts, though described with poetic hyperbole for are they not the vanguard of the Day of God's judgment?-are real locusts; their plague has just been felt by his contemporaries, whom he summons to repent, and to whom, when they have repented, he promises the restoration of their ruined harvests, the outpouring of the Spirit, and judgment on their foes. Prediction is thus found only in the second half of the book (2:18 onwards): it rests on the basis of narrative and exhortation which fills the first half.3 

Those who hold to the allegorical interpretation argue that Joel's language about the locust plague is entirely too aggravated and ominous to represent a natural plague. Such plagues occur at intervals in Palestine and it is not to be expected that Joel would connect such a common adversity with the crisis of the Day of the Lord. 

Using the figure of the locusts he surely must be describing a more awful agency of God's wrath. In fact, from his description, one might be justified in saying that it seems to imply a human army. The latter represents part or all of the heathen nations that have been hostile to 

3George Adam Smith, The Book of the Twelve Prophets (New and 

Revised Ed.), II, 382.

Israel in different periods of her long history. 

Those who uphold this theory are divided, however, as to whether the heathen nations symbolized by the locusts have already attacked Israel or whether the attacks are yet in the future.

Certain individuals who hold to the third view, while admitting certain features of the allegorical interpretation to be untenable, are still not readily disposed to believe with the first group that the forepart of Joel is narrative and hence historical. They agree that the prophet has in mind a locust plague, but hold that it is still in the future. They think it is as much in the future as the pouring out of the spirit upon all flesh¹ or of the judgment of God upon the nations. All these features are alike signs of the Day of the Lord, and that Day is yet to "The prophet's scenery is apocalyptic; the locusts are 'eschatological' locusts,' not historical." come. So much for the usual interpretations of Joel. Again, one may ask as we did in the case of Hosea's marriage, Which of the interpretations given above is correctassuming any is correct? But in the case of the prophecy of Joel we have every reason to believe that a modern American prophet, Joseph Smith, received a clue to its interpretation from the lips of a resurrected American prophet whose name was Moroni. Let us, therefore, consider the divine messenger's words.

MORONI'S CLUE TO THE INTERPRETATION OF 

JOEL.-When the glorified, resurrected being known to us as Moroni appeared to the prophet Joseph Smith on 

4Joel 2:28. 

5 Joel 3:5.. 

6That is, in the nature of a revelation. "Referring to the doctrine of the last or final things, as death, judgment, heaven, and hell.

the evening of September 21, 1823, he quoted and explained several Old Testament prophecies, including part of Joel. In writing of this event Joseph Smith says:

He (Moroni) also quoted the second chapter of Joel, from the twenty-eighth verse to the last. He also said that this was not yet fulfilled, but was soon to be.8

The careful student will at once recognize that these are important words and should certainly prove useful in the interpretation of the first part of Joel. It will be appropriate for us to look in some detail at the passage quoted by Moroni. Of interest is the fact that it forms a separate chapter by itself in the Hebrew Bible. 

And it shall come to pass afterward, That I will pour out My spirit upon all flesh; 

And your sons and your daughters shall prophesy, Your old men shall dream dreams, Your young men shall see visions; 

And also upon the servants and upon the handmaids

In those days will I pour out My spirit. 

And I will show wonders in the heavens and in the earth, Blood, and fire, and pillars of smoke. 

The sun shall be turned into darkness, And the moon into blood, Before the great and terrible day of the Lord come.

And it shall come to pass, that whosoever shall call on the name of the Lord shall be delivered;

For in mount Zion and in Jerusalem there shall be those that escape, As the Lord hath said, And among the remnant those whom the Lord shall call.⁹

This part of Joel's prophecy, let us keep in mind, was in Joseph Smith's time "not yet fulfilled, but was 

8D. H. C., I, 13. 

9Joel 2:28-32. 1

soon to be." So had said the divine messenger. This revelation gives us solid ground upon which to stand in interpreting the prophecy. I take it that the above scripture means substantially the following: There is a time coming in the near future when the Spirit of God, now withdrawn in great measure from the world because of wickedness, shall be poured out upon all flesh. This great pentecostal era can, of course, come only after the wicked have had the judgments of God poured out upon them without measure. The Spirit does not abide in the midst of wickedness. In the period just preceding "the great and terrible day of the Lord"-terrible because it is a climax of judgment-certain cosmic signs shall be vouchsafed men as warnings that God's judgments are at hand. 

Let us inquire as to the exact meaning of the phrase, “the great and terrible day of the Lord," used by Joel and in modified form by others of the prophets. Once established it will be of great help, not only in interpreting Joel, but the others as well. In several revelations given by the Lord to Joseph Smith the matter is made clear.

In September, 1830, the prophet was commanded to bring about a gathering of the Lord's elect, "for mine elect hear my voice and harden not their heart."10 The 

Lord continues: 

Wherefore the decree hath gone forth from the Father that they shall be gathered in unto one place upon the face of this land, to prepare their hearts and be prepared in all things against the day when tribulation and desolation are sent forth upon the wicked. For the hour is nigh and the day soon at hand when the earth is ripe; and all the proud and they that do wickedly shall be as stubble; and I will burn them up, saith the Lord of Hosts, that wickedness shall not be upon the earth; for the hour is nigh, 10D. & C. 29:7.

and that which was spoken by mine apostles must be fulfilled; for as they spoke so shall it come to pass; for I will reveal myself from heaven with power and great glory, with all the hosts thereof, and dwell in righteousness with men on earth a thousand years, and the wicked shall not stand. . . . But, behold, I say unto you that before this great day shall come the sun shall be darkened, and the moon shall be turned into blood, and the stars shall fall from heaven, and there shall be greater signs in heaven above and in the earth beneath; and there shall be weeping and wailing among the hosts of men. .. (italics ours).11 ...

A careful study of this scripture must convince one that the Second Advent of the Christ is equivalent to the "great and terrible day of the Lord." The reader should carefully note the italicized passages which must be direct allusions to the passage in Joel we are now expounding. 

Attention is also called to two other revelations. The first, D. & C. 43:17-30, is very explicit and commands all nations to repent and "prepare yourselves for the great day of the Lord." The Lord goes on to explain that "in mine own due time will I come upon the earth in judgment, and my people shall be redeemed and shall reign with me on earth." The other revelation, D. & C. 45:1559, contains the instructions given by the Savior to His ancient disciples concerning His advent in glory. Note especially the following: 

And it shall come to pass that he that feareth me shall be looking forth for the great day of the Lord to come, even for the signs of the coming of the Son of Man. And they shall see signs and wonders, for they shall be shown forth in the heavens above, and in the earth beneath. And they shall behold blood, and fire, and vapors of smoke. And before the day of the Lord shall come, the sun shall be darkened, and the moon be turned into blood, and the stars fall from heaven. And the remnant shall be gathered unto this place (italics ours).12 

11D. & C. 29:8-11, 14, 15. 

12D. & C. 45:39-43.

Here once more is a view that the Day of the Lord is the Second Advent. And the reader will note again the unmistakable allusions (in italics) to the passage under consideration in Joel. The Lord seems not to have forgotten His ancient servant who foresaw the great events of our generation.

Having now established the meaning of the expression under consideration we may continue to expound the rest of the scripture with which we began. Joel points out that those who call on the Lord's name in the day of His judgment "shall be delivered." I do not feel as many scholars do that Joel meant this in a narrow sense -that only Israelites should be saved. Such a view is too narrow to impute to the great prophet who saw so far down the centuries of time to the Lord's coming. If no Gentiles are saved it will only be because they have stubbornly refused to heed God's call to repentance.

When the prophet says that "in Mount Zion and in Jerusalem there shall be those that escape" he may be making special reference to Israelites on the American continent13-Joseph Smith said it was the land of Zion -as well as to Jews and others of Israel in Palestine. 

Finally, Joel makes reference to "the remnant"'14 that should be saved. This evidently refers to the remnant of Israel, spoken of by Isaiah, and others of Jacob's descendants who should be saved. It may be the “remnant” spoken of in D. & C. 45:43 and quoted above.

Such, in brief, is my interpretation of the passage from Joel quoted by Moroni to Joseph Smith. It may be summed up as follows: 1. The pouring out of the 

13In D. & C. 84:2 the Lord speaks of "Mount Zion, which shall be the city of New Jerusalem." See also 45:65-70 and 133:8-13, 18-24. These passages are helpful in interpreting Joel and others of the prophets. 

14 Joel 2:32.

Lord's spirit in the latter days, 2. Signs to be given in heaven and on earth of coming judgments, 3. The Second

Advent of the Lord in judgment, 4. The righteous, especially the "remnant" or "elect" of Israel, to be delivered.

My belief is that this passage is practically an epitome- -a summary or condensation of the whole Book of Joel.

The first part of Joel, chapters 1:2-2:27, now claims our attention.

THE HARBINGER OF THE DAY OF THE LORD.-As pointed out above, Joel, in ominous and aggravated figures of a locust plague, drought, and fire, pictures the events preceding the "great and terrible day of the Lord." Note the opening lines in which the fear of something more than an ordinary scourge of locusts is played up: 

Hear this, ye old men, And give ear, all ye inhabitants of the land.15 

Hath this been in your days, Or in the days of your fathers? 

Tell ye your children of it, And let your children tell their children, And their children another generation. 

That which the palmer-worm hath left hath the locust eaten; 

And that which the locust hath left hath the canker-worm eaten;

And that which the canker-worm hath left hath the caterpillar eaten. 

Awake, ye drunkards, and weep, And wail, all ye drinkers of wine, Because of the sweet wine, For it is cut off from your mouth.

For a people is come up upon my land, Mighty, and without number; 

15 The Hebrew word eretz is justly translated land in this line, but it also has the meaning earth, which would fit our interpretation of the prophecy as given in the text.

His teeth are the teeth of a lion 

And he hath the jaw-teeth of a lioness.

He hath laid my vine waste, And blasted my fig-tree;

He hath made it clean bare, and cast it down, The branches thereof are made white.16

The terribly destructive effects of locust plagues in Palestine are well known to every student of the subject. 

The reader will find it profitable to study Mr. John D. 

Whiting's description of the great locust plage of 1915 in the National Geographic Magazine for December of that year. Other plagues are known to have occurred in the years 1845, 1865, 1892, 1899, and 1904. Joel does not exaggerate when he describes the destructive effects of locusts on vines, fig-trees, and other vegetation. But while the prophet uses the very real imagery of a plague whose effects are well known to him, he makes a much broader application of it than just to his own land and people. 

To him the locusts represent general devastation and destruction upon the whole earth. If he means an ordinary locust plague upon his own land, why does he ask the question: 

Hath this been in your days, Or in the days of your fathers?

The expected answer is "no," whereas to an ordinary plague the answer must have been "yes," because such locust scourges were well known in Joel's day. But let us not forget the key left us by Moroni. Our point that the scourge of locusts represents a general judgment upon the whole earth is borne out when Joel uses certain key expressions in a further description of destruction by means of drought and fire. 16 Joel 1:2-7.

Alas for the day! 

For the day of the Lord is at hand, And as a destruction from the Almighty shall it come. 

Is not the food cut off before our eyes, Yea, joy and gladness 

From the house of our God? 

The grains shrivel under their hoes;

The garners are laid desolate, The barns are broken down; 

For the grain is withered. 

Unto Thee, O Lord, do I cry; 

For the fire hath devoured 

The pastures of the wilderness, And the flame hath set ablaze 

All the trees of the field.

Yea, the beasts of the field pant unto Thee; 

For the water brooks are dried up, And the fire hath devoured the pastures of the wilderness.17 

Note the lines having a familiar ring: 

Alas for the day! 

For the day of the Lord is at hand...

We have already learned that the Day of the Lord as explained by Moroni to Joseph Smith meant the Second 

Advent of the Savior in the latter days to judgment. Can there be any doubt, then, that Joel is here speaking of the judgments of God upon the earth prior to that event?

Also the strong and unusual language used by the prophet in describing the drought and fire must strike one with wonder. 

Then again in chapter 2:1-11 another description of the destruction is given in somewhat different, but still vivid word pictures. May we call attention to two passages from this section. 

17 Joel 1:15-17, 19, 20.

Blow ye the horn in Zion, And sound an alarm in My holy mountain; 

Let all the inhabitants of the land 18 tremble; 

For the day of the Lord cometh, For it is at hand; 

A day of darkness and gloominess, A day of clouds and thick darkness, As blackness spread upon the mountains.¹ 19 Here again we have the Day of the Lord mentioned as before. And in the second passage the same thing is spoken of in foreboding language: 

And the Lord uttereth His voice before His army; 

And His camp is very great, For He is mighty that executeth His word; 

For great is the day of the Lord and very terrible; 

And who can abide it?20

In the mind of the writer no doubt remains that Joel foresaw the dispensation in which we live and God's judgments upon the world. This he expressed in figures that would be easily understood by his people. So acutely and painfully were the judgments that Joel saw impressed upon his mind that he cried out in anguish—as if present -to the people of our day to repent and escape God's wrath. Thus his prophecy is an ever-present warning to us. 

Yet even now, saith the Lord, Turn ye unto Me with all your heart, And with fasting, and with weeping, and with lamentation; 

And rend your heart, and not your garments, And turn unto the Lord your God;

For He is gracious and compassionate, 18See note 15. 19 Joel 2:1, 2. 

20 Joel 2:11. Cf. Mal. 3:2.

Long-suffering, and abundant in mercy, And repenteth Him of the evil. 

Who knoweth whether He will not turn and repent, And leave a blessing behind him?21

JOEL'S PLEA FOR ISRAEL.-So concerned is the prophet over God's judgments upon the world that he makes a special plea for Israel. In language containing allusions to religious customs of his own day he says: 

Let the priests, the ministers of the Lord, Weep between the porch and the altar, And let them say: "Spare Thy people, O Lord, And give not Thy heritage to reproach, That the nations should make them a byword: 

Wherefore should they say among the peoples: 

Where is their God?"22

THE LORD'S ANSWER.-The scene changes and Joel foresees that God will answer the petition and bless His people. Prosperity shall eventually attend them; their enemies shall be removed; the desolation and destruction typified by the locust plague shall be made up to them; the Lord shall be in their midst and they shall never be ashamed. 

Then was the Lord jealous for His land, And had pity on His people. 

And the Lord answered and said unto His people: "Behold, I will send you grain, and wine, and oil, And ye shall be satisfied therewith; 

And I will no more make you a reproach among the nations; 

But I will remove far off from you the northern one," 

And will drive him into a land barren and desolate.” 

21Joel 2:12-14. 22 Joel 2:17. 23 23 The allusion to the northern one is somewhat obscure. The Authorized 

Version translators rendered the Hebrew equivalent as northern army. "Army" does not, however, appear in the original. At any rate the Hebrew represents the instruments of the Lord's wrath in the day of His judgment.

Fear not, O land, be glad and rejoice; 

For the Lord hath done great things. 

Be not afraid, ye beasts of the field;

For the pastures of the wilderness do spring, For the tree beareth its fruit,

The fig-tree and the vine do yield their strength. 

Be glad then, ye children of Zion, and rejoice 

In the Lord your God. 

And I will restore to you the years that the locust hath eaten,

The canker-worm, and the caterpillar, and the palmer-worm, My great army which I sent among you. 

And ye shall eat in plenty and be satisfied, And shall praise the name of the Lord your God, That hath dealt wondrously with you; 

And My people shall never be ashamed. 

And ye shall know that I am in the midst of Israel, And that I am the Lord your God, and there is none else; 

And My people shall never be ashamed.2 24 The above is an idealized picture of Israel's future. 

And by Israel I have reference to all her branches, whether the Jew in Palestine, the Ten Tribes, or Ephraim on this continent. The events of our day are constantly clarifying to us the picture drawn of old by Joel. 

JUDGMENT IN THE VALLEY OF JEHOSHAPHAT.- 

We have seen in our study of the first two chapters of Joel how the prophet, using allusions and figures well understood by his people, describes the great signs and judgments to take place in the latter days before the 

Advent of our Lord. Also that the prophet foresees the blessings of God upon Israel during that trying time. 

In chapter 3, Joel gives another picture of God's judgment upon the nations. Israel, whom he foresees will be scat- 24 Joel 2:18-23, 25-27.

tered among the nations, is to receive a change in her fortunes and retribution will come upon her adversaries in the valley of Jehoshaphat. The inspired writer in telling of the judgments recalls injustices done to Israel presumably in his own lifetime or before. Boys and girls have been sold into captivity in exchange for harlots or a drink of wine. 

For, behold, in those days, and in that time,25

When I shall bring back the captivity of Judah and Jerusalem, I will gather all nations,

And will bring them down into the valley of Jehoshaphat; 

And I will enter into judgment with them there For My people and for My heritage Israel, Whom they have scattered among the nations, And divided My land. 

And they have cast lots for My people; 

And have given a boy for a harlot, And sold a girl for wine, and have drunk.26

It is possible that in Joel's day many Israelites had been scattered to distant countries, but it was not until Roman times that lots were cast upon the nation as a whole, to sell the people as slaves. After the conquest and destruction of Jerusalem, the Roman leader Titus disposed of the prisoners under seventeen years of age by publicly selling them. Others were executed, some were sent to work in Egyptian mines, others fought wild beasts in 

Roman shows; only the tallest and most handsome ones were reserved for the triumphal procession in Rome.

The retribution that is to be wrought by God upon the nations in the valley of Jehoshaphat deserves a word of comment. Many authorities have supposed that Joel actually meant the narrow valley of the Kidron, between 

25 That is, the latter days. 26 Joel 3:1-3.

Jerusalem and the Mount of Olives, to be the place of judgment. Jews for centuries have made a burying-place of parts of the valley and have maintained that the final world judgment will be held there. Other authorities have held that the very magnitude of the subject precludes the possibility that Jehoshaphat is a place name. They point out that the valley of Jehoshaphat means literally the valley where Jehovah judgeth, and is probably symbolical in import. It should be noted also that in 3:14 the valley is spoken of as the valley of decision. Which of these views is the correct one may not be so important to us as the fact that judgment is to come. But the attention of the reader should be called to two passages in the Doctrine and Covenants that may have reference to the event mentioned by Joel. The first occurs in Section 45:47-49. 

Then shall the arm of the Lord fall upon the nations. And then shall the Lord set his foot upon this mount, and it shall cleave in twain, and the earth shall tremble, and reel to and fro, and the heavens also shall shake. And the Lord shall utter his voice, and all the ends of the earth shall hear it; and the nations of the earth shall mourn, and they that have laughed shall see their folly.

This passage refers to the "mount" which is, as the context shows, the Mount of Olivet. There may be also an allusion to Joel 3:16 which deals with the Day of the 

Lord in the valley of decision. The second passage occurs in Section 133:19-21. 

Wherefore, prepare ye for the coming of the Bridegroom; go ye, go ye out to meet him. For behold, he shall stand upon the Mount of Olivet, and upon the mighty ocean, even the great deep, and upon the islands of the sea, and upon the land of Zion. 

And he shall utter his voice out of Zion, and he shall speak from Jerusalem, and his voice shall be heard among all people.

It will be noticed that this scripture mentions the Mount of Olivet, but mentions also other far distant places. Thus the idea of the place of judgment would seem to be greatly extended. There is a definite allusion to Joel 3:16 in this passage. It seems to the writer that these passages in the Doctrine and Covenants refer to the same general subject of the judgment scenes dealt with in the third chapter of Joel. The definite allusion to Joel 

3:16 in D. & C. 133:21 seems to make this decision certain.

In verses 4-8, 19 of this chapter, Joel depicts the retribution that shall come upon Tyre, Zidon, Philistia, Egypt, and Edom because of the hostility manifested towards his people in his own time and because of the acts of violence they have perpetrated. These powers carried off gold and silver and other treasures which they placed in their own temples; they sold Israelitish children to the 

Greeks (Jevanim), and in other ways demonstrated their unrighteous attitudes. Joel's predictions have been mostly fulfilled through the centuries; those which have not yet been fulfilled we may assume will be in the judgment scenes he describes concerning the latter days.

Verses 9-17 contain a moving and powerful description of the fulfillment of the judgment upon all the nations predicted in verse 2. As Professor C. F. Keil writes:

The call is addressed to all nations to equip themselves for battle, and march into the valley of Jehoshaphat to war against the people of God, but in reality to be judged by the Lord through 

His heavenly heroes (mighty ones), whom He sends down thither. . . . It is to the last decisive judgment, in which all the single judgments find their end, that the command of Jehovah to His strong heroes refers. . . . The judgment is represented under the double figure of the reaping of the fields and the tread-

ing out of the grapes in the wine-press. . . .The ripeness of the grain is a figurative representation of ripeness of judgment.27 

The enemies of God are destroyed, but He will prove a refuge and strong tower to His people. 

Proclaim ye this among the nations, Prepare war; 

Stir up the mighty men; 

Let all the men of war draw near, Let them come up. 

Beat your ploughshares into swords, And your pruning-hooks into spears; 

Let the weak say: "I am strong." 

Haste ye, and come, all ye nations round about, And gather yourselves together; 

Thither cause Thy mighty ones to come down, O Lord!

Let the nations be stirred up, and come up 

To the valley of Jehoshaphat; 

For there will I sit to judge 

All the nations round about. 

Put ye in the sickle,

For the harvest is ripe; 

Come, tread ye; 

For the winepress is full, the vats overflow; 

For their wickedness is great. 

Multitudes, multitudes in the valley of decision!

For the day of the Lord is near in the valley of decision. 

The sun and the moon are become black, And the stars withdraw their shining. 

And the Lord shall roar from Zion, And utter His voice from Jerusalem, And the heavens and the earth shall shake;

But the Lord will be a refuge unto His people, And a stronghold to the children of Israel. 

So shall ye know that I am the Lord your God, 27C. F. Keil and F. Delitzsch, Biblical Commentary on the Old Testament 

(The Twelve Minor Prophets), I, 225-227.

Dwelling in Zion My holy mountain; 

Then shall Jerusalem be holy, And there shall no strangers pass through her anymore.28

Joel ends his prophecy by showing that after God's judgment upon the nations has taken place, Israel shall be blessed and prospered "from generation to generation." 

In figures typical of the Hebrew genius he has the "mountains drop down sweet wine" and the "hills flow with milk," which express how fruitful the Lord's land shall be. 

And it shall come to pass in that day, That the mountains shall drop down sweet wine, And the hills shall flow with milk,

And all the brooks of Judah shall flow with waters; 

And a fountain shall come forth of the house of the Lord, And shall water the valley of Shittim.

But Judah shall be inhabited for ever, And Jerusalem from generation to generation.

But I will hold as innocent their blood that I have not held as innocent; 29 

And the Lord dwelleth in Zion.30

The Book of Joel must ever remain to the Mormon people one of the most important prophecies of the Old Testament. The fact that part of it was quoted by 

Moroni, the holy messenger from God, to the young prophet Joseph Smith at the beginning of this dispensation emphasizes the point. The world, we fear, may take its "voice of warning" to heart too late, because its "apocalyptic" nature is held too cheaply at the present time. 

28 Joel 3:9-17. 

29There is some question as to the meaning of this line. Some think it has reference to the nations of the world-that the desolation coming upon them will atone for the wrong they have done to God's people. Others think it has reference to the Jews. 

30 Joel 3:18, 20, 21.

CHAPTER IV 

E 

AMOS-PROPHET OF SOCIAL JUSTICE ARLY LIFE AND TIMES OF THE PROPHET.—The name of the prophet means "burden-bearer" or "burdened". The reader is cautioned against confusing the name of Amos with that of Amoz, the father of Isaiah the prophet. The early life of Amos, and for aught we know, most of his life, was spent at Tekoa, a small town located on a hill at the edge of the Judean plateau six miles south of Bethlehem. The writer spent considerable time at this place and a more desolate site for a town or village can scarcely be imagined. It is necessary to walk the six miles from Bethlehem or ride one of the poor donkeys that can be hired for a small sum from Arab owners. Little remains of the buildings that were constructed at various times on the site of the town. To the south, north, and west limestone hills obscure the view and eastward the even more barren desert stretches out for eighteen miles to the Dead Sea. Here

Amos, one of the greatest religious figures of all times, herded sheep (an ugly variety known as noked, but prized for their fine wool) and dressed sycamore trees that produced a small inferior fig, having a sweet but watery taste. His shepherd profession made him appreciate the open country with its freshness and vigor, as compared with the cities to which he doubtless carried wool. The prophecy under his name indicates that he was a very thoughtful and discerning observer of the religious and social state of his times. The superscription of his book sets forth that Amos entered on his ministry "in the days of Uzziah, king of Judah, and in the days of Jeroboam,

the son of Joash, king of Israel, two years before the earthquake." We do not know exactly when the earthquake -it must have been severe-took place, but we can, with some confidence, date Amos at about 750 B. C.

Israel, the kingdom to the north of Judah against which his preaching was mainly directed, was prosperous -yes, even wealthy.¹ Greed, corruption, and vice reigned supreme in the so-called upper classes of society. The social lot of the poor classes was pitiful. Religion had little real vitality and the dishonesty, insincerity, and blindness of public officials made it possible for the strong to exploit the weak. Morals seemed all but forgotten. Into such a society walked the prophet of Tekoa and denounced it with such vigor, clarity of thought, and authority that his message is still eagerly read and applied to modern situations. In the chapter on Hosea, who was a contemporary of Amos, we have given a more complete account of the social conditions of the time and to it the reader is referred.

THE CALL OF AMOS TO THE MINISTRY.—It is not until the seventh chapter of Amos is reached that we learn the circumstances under which the prophet was called to the ministry. There we glean the information that he was called from his flock to prophesy against Israel. 

Then answered Amos, and said to Amaziah: "I was no prophet, neither was I a prophet's son; but I was a herdman, and a cultivator of sycamore-figs; and the Lord took me from the following the flock, and the Lord said unto me: Go, prophesy unto My people Israel."2

The prophet went as he was instructed from his 1The prosperity was very similar to that in America during and after the first World War. 

2Amos 7:14, 15.

native Judean home to Bethel, the sanctuary of the court of Israel, the Northern Kingdom, to deliver the message the Lord confided to him. Fearlessly he delivered it in forceful, classical Hebrew-the best in the Old Testament. 

THE CONTENT AND STYLE OF THE BOOK.-The prophecy is orderly and methodical in arrangement; it proceeds on the basis of a logical sequence rather than a chronological one. It falls quite naturally into three divisions: 1. Chapters 1 and 2, in the nature of a pro logue containing threats of coming judgment against eight nations, including Judah, and more especially, Israel. 

2. Chapters 3 to 6, a number of discourses giving the grounds for God's judgments. 3. Chapters 7 to 9, containing a series of five visions of the execution of the judgment, interrupted by a historical section and explanatory remarks on the same subjects that are discussed in chapters 3 to 6. This division ends with a short description of the eventual redemption and glorification of the remnant of Israel.

A more detailed scheme of study for the benefit of the student is presented herewith.

I. The Prologue: Threats of coming judgment against the nations (1:2-2:16). 

Preface: The Lord's terrible manifestation (1:2 cf. Joel 

3:16).

A. The transgressions and punishments of six foreign nations (1:3-2:3). 

B. The transgressions and punishment of Judah (2:4, 5).

C. The transgressions and punishment of Israel (2:6-16). 

II. The grounds for God's judgments on Israel (3:1-6:14). 

A. Condemnation of Israel's ruling classes (3:1-4:3). 

B.

Israel fails to understand the Lord's judgments 

---

C. Lamentations, exhortations, reproofs, and threats of ruin (5:1-17).

D. Woe and terror upon those who desire the day of the 

Lord (5:18-27).

E. Woe upon the careless rich, the self-satisfied, and arrogant (6:1-14). 

III. Five visions of the execution of the Lord's judgments, with interludes (7:1-9:15). 

A. Three visions: The locusts, the fire, the plumbline 

B. Interlude: The experience of Amos at Bethel 

(7:10-17). 

C. The fourth vision: The basket of summer fruit (8:1-3). 

D. Interlude: Denunciation, judgment, effects of the judgment (8:4-14).

E. The fifth vision: The smitten sanctuary (9:1-6). 

F. The Lord respects character, not race (9:7-10).

G. Epilogue: The eventual redemption and glorifica tion of Israel (9:11-15).

The style of Amos is finished and refined. The prophet does, indeed, betray his shepherd's profession in the use of certain words, which apparently belonged to the language of the common people, and in many figures and similes drawn from nature and rural life; but on the other hand, he shows an intimate acquaintance with the Mosaic law and his nation's history, and displays much rhetorical power, wealth and depth of thought, animation and vigor, especially in the use of bold antitheses, and a genuine poetical roll, which rises somewhat frequently into actual rhythm.

In point of style, Amos holds no mean place among the prophets. The declaration of Jerome, that he was imperitus sermone (rude in speech), has not been justified by modern critics. On the contrary, it is usually allowed that, though destitute of sublimity, he is distinguished for perspicuity and regu-

larity, embellishment and elegance, energy and fullness. His images are mostly original, and taken from the natural scenery with which he was familiar; his rhythmus is smooth and flowing; and his parallelisms are in a high degree natural and complete. In description, he is for the most part special and local; he excells in the minuteness of his groupings, while the general vividness of his manner imparts a more intense interest to all that he delivers. In some few instances, as in chapters IV, VI and VII, the language approaches more to the prose style, or is entirely that of narrative.3

Amos, as has been intimated, writes the most pure and classical Hebrew in the Old Testament. This fact surprises many folk, for they can't understand how a shepherd could produce such a fine piece of literature. 

In Eastern society superior culture is not uncommon in connection with the poverty of shepherd life. “At the courts of the 

Caliphs and their Emirs the rude Arabs of the desert were wont to appear without any feeling of awkwardness, and to surprise the courtiers by the finish of their impromptu verses, the fluent eloquence of their oratory, and the range of subjects on which they could speak with knowledge and discrimination. Among the Hebrews, as in the Arabian desert, knowledge and oratory were not affairs of professional education, or dependent for their cultivaiton on wealth and social status. The sum of book-learning was small; men of all ranks mingled with that Oriental freedom which is so foreign to our habits; shrewd observation, a memory retentive of traditional lore, and the faculty of original reflection took the place of laborious study as the ground of acknowledged intellectual pre-eminence."

It might be added that the Holy Spirit must have aided the prophets of the Lord remarkably in giving messages that would otherwise have suffered without it. 

3E. Henderson, The Book of the Twelve Prophets, 2nd Ed., p. 127. 

4"Amos," International Critical Commentary, Intro., CVII.

The Hebrew text of Amos seems, on the whole, to be fairly well preserved. Occasionally the Septuagint helps us to emend passages in the Hebrew that are corrupt. 

THE PROLOGUE: THE LORD ROARS FROM ZION.- 

The first division, dealing with the Lord's threats against the nations, is masterly in its conception, and execution. 

The prophet adopts a theme, "The Lord roars from Zion and utters His voice from Jerusalem," from Joel 3:16, which refers to the judgments of the latter days, and applies it to God's coming judgments on eight nations, including his own country Judah, but, more especially, Israel. In the words of Professor McFadyen:

The clear eyes of Amos saw the symptoms of rottenness and inevitable decay; and the words of his first recorded message are that Jehovah, the God of this easy-going people, would roar from His temple in Jerusalem, like a lion just before he makes his spring. The implication is that Jehovah will soon spring upon 

His people, to tear them in pieces; and Amos's message we might describe as the Gospel of the Lion's Roar." 

The prophet spins a web about his listeners and at the same time holds their interest by first pronouncing

God's judgments upon successive nations outside of Israel. 

The nations are Damascus (Syria), Philistia, Phoenicia, Edom, Ammon, Moab, and Judah.' Amos begins each of his denunciations with the words: "Thus saith the 

Lord, for three transgressions, yea, for four, I will not reverse it." By "three transgressions" is meant much sin.

One sin is then specified out of many to justify the coming judgment upon each nation. Amos's words thrill and delight his audience, particularly his pronouncements upon 

5The Greek version of the Old Testament. 

6John E. McFadyen, A Cry for Justice, p. 2. 

7The student is advised to locate these countries in succession upon a map. Note the web Amos spins around Israel.

Judah, its hated rival kingdom. But suddenly, as a bolt from the blue, Amos turns upon his audience and the nations it represents. He stalks Israel as a lion does its prey. Israel, too, is as guilty of misconduct as the nations before-mentioned. The wealthy mistreat the poor and humble, prostitution is rampant, the people are winebibbers, prophets are disregarded, and young men are made to break their covenants.8 But retribution is to come upon the guilty nation. God will strike and the strong shall not be delivered.9

LAW REIGNS IN THE REALM OF THE SPIRIT.—In this day of scientific method and observation we are far too prone to assume that while law and order reign in the physical domain it is questionable that they do so in the realm of spiritual values. It is to the eternal honor of 

Amos that he perceived law reigning as inexorably in the realm of the spirit as it does in the physical world. Amos applied his observation with characteristic vigor and clarity to the position of Israel before the Lord. 

Hear this word that the Lord hath spoken against you, O children of Israel, against the whole family which I brought up out of the land of Egypt, saying: 

You only have I known of all the families of the earth; 

Therefore I will visit upon you all your iniquities.10

The two poetic lines above form one of the grandest passages in all scripture. In the Hebrew scriptures, "to know" often signifies "to care for," both with the understanding and the heart. Thus Amos pointed out the unique role God had assigned to Israel in the world. Amos understood that Israel was the "chosen" people of God 8 Amos 2:6-12. 

9Amos 2:13-16. 10 Amos 3:1, 2.

in the sense that it was a covenant people. But we can easily imagine Amos saying:

Special privilege implies corresponding responsibility, O Israel, and you have not measured up to it. "There is a law, irrevocably decreed in heaven before the foundations of this world, upon which all blessings are predicated-and when we obtain any blessing from God, it is by obedience to that law upon which it is predicated."11 

As Professor McFadyen has written:

They (Israel) believed in their election without understanding the reasons for it; they failed to realize that election to privilege is always election to duty and responsibility.¹ 12 Amos then proceeded to clinch, by a series of homely illustrations from the physical world, his point that law also reigns in the realm of spirit. 

Will two walk together, Except they have agreed? 

Will a lion roar in the forest, When he hath no prey?

Will a young lion give forth his voice out of his den, If he have taken nothing? 

Will a bird fall in a snare upon the earth, When there is no lure for it? 

Will a snare spring up from the ground, And have taken nothing at all? 

Shall the horn be blown in a city, And the people not tremble? 

Shall evil befall a city, And the Lord hath not done it?13

The answer to each of these questions is "No". The great law of cause and effect is apparent in the world of 

11See D. & C. 130:20, 21. 

12McFadyen, op. cit., p. 23. 

13 Amos 3:3-6.

material things. There is little or nothing we know of that is isolated or haphazard: for every phenomenon there is a cause and a rational explanation. As it is in the physical world so in the world of spiritual values—as a nation sows so shall it also reap. "Therefore," said 

Amos to Israel, "I will visit upon you all your iniquities." 

THE LORD REVEALS HIS COUNSEL TO HIS SER- 

VANTS THE PROPHETS.-Amos points out to Israel that his message to the nation, too, has a cause: he appears because the Lord has confided in him and has revealed to His servant the coming doom of Israel. Prophets are the men unto whom God gives counsel and revelation and woe to the nation that heeds not what they say. 

For the Lord God will do nothing, But he revealeth His counsel unto His servants the prophets. 

The lion hath roared, Who will not fear? 

The Lord God hath spoken, Who can but prophesy?14

WOMEN DETERMINE THE TEMPER AND QUALITY OF CIVILIZATION.-Amos must have been a man of su perb courage and daring. He boldly continued that part of his address dealing with the grounds for God's coming judgments by giving a scathing rebuke to women in high places. Many of our English translations are too elegant to reveal effectually Amos's contempt for the women of Israel who were helping to make its social life cruel and rotten and its religion a gorgeous sham. What he actually said was: 

Hear this word, you cows of Bashan, Who are on the Mount of Samaria, Who oppress the poor, who crush the needy, Who say to their lords: "Bring, and let us drink.” 

14 Amos 3:7, 8.

The Lord Jehovah has sworn by His holiness: 

Behold, days come upon you, That He will carry you away with hooks, And the remnant of you with fish-hooks. 

And you shall go out at the breaches, every one straight before her, And you shall be cast into Harmon, Saith the Lord.15

Amos compares the women of Samaria with the fat and well-fed cows roaming on pastures to the east of the 

Sea of Galilee. These rich, voluptuous, and violent women are qualified partners for their lords whom Amos has already denounced, who "store up violence and robbery in their palaces."'16 The sin of these sleek women consisted in their tyrannical oppression of the poor people in that they requested their husbands to procure them wine bought with money squeezed from their victims. 

A country is largely what its women make it; if they are cruel or careless or unwomanly, the country is on the road to ruin. 

But these cattle on the hills of Samaria at whom Amos flings his words of scorn are worse than the cattle on the hills of Bashan; for they have done what no animal could do they have made coarse pleasure the deliberate end of life. . . . They are fit partners for the lords already denounced. . . . Drink, as we have seen, was one of the national perils of the day (2:8, 12), and no sight can be uglier than a drunken woman. Intemperance and cruelty went together then, as they go so often still. When women, who should be pitiful, sink to such depths of shame and heartlessness, it must be made plain that God will soon appear to mete out to them what they had meted to the poor.17

Like so many fish pulled out of their element with fish-hooks, these women with all their luxury would 15 Amos 4:1-3. 

16 Amos 3:10. 

17McFadyen, op. cit., pp. 36f.

be taken in hand by Israel's enemies. They would be insulted, abused, and cast around like old rags. In sorrow and shame they would realize that repentance came too late.

EVERY DISASTER A NEW CALL TO REPENTANCE.- 

Amos continued to castigate Israel by exhorting his audience in ironical vein to continue its heartless ceremonial worship. 

Come to Beth-el, and transgress, To Gilgal, and multiply transgression; 

And bring your sacrifices in the morning, And your tithes after three days; 

And offer a sacrifice of thanksgiving of that which is leavened,

And proclaim freewill-offerings and publish them; 

For so ye love to do, O ye children of Israel, Saith the Lord God.18

Israel was meticulous in its performance of the outward requirements of its religion, but the inner and less tangible requirements of love, mercy, justice, and humility were either not understood or were disregarded. In an endeavor to bring His people to their senses the Lord, said Amos, had sent upon them seven natural calamities. 

Cleanness of teeth,19 drought, blasting, mildew, insect pests, pestilence, death by the sword, and burning were brought in succession, but all to no avail.20 Amos's heart was bleeding over the sinful state of Israel. He could do nothing but warn the nation of the final blow which God would send and for which the people must prepare themselves.21 It was no pleasure for him to pronounce judg-

ment upon his brethren. So he took up a lamentation over them:

The virgin of Israel is fallen, She shall no more rise; 

She is cast down upon her land, There is none to raise her up. 

For thus saith the Lord God: The city that went forth a thousand shall have a hundred left, and that which went forth a hundred shall have ten left, of the house of Israel.22

When God threatens he is promising . . . when he comes near in any way it is for our salvation . . . God is not obliged to fulfill his threats, but he is obliged to fulfill his promises." 24

Seek good, and not evil, that ye may live; 

And so the Lord, the God of hosts, will be with you, as ye say. 

Hate the evil, and love the good, And establish justice in the gate;

It may be that the Lord, the God of hosts, Will be gracious unto the remnant of Joseph.25 

Note Amos' emphasis on the word live. 

He used the word here in much the same sense as the Syriac version of the New Testament uses the word life. Life stands equally for salvation, and to be saved is to live. Amos had a passion for social justice. Goodness to Amos had a strong social color-he wanted justice in society and fair play between man and man.

THE TERRORS OF THE DAY OF THE LORD.—The prophet then paints a doleful picture of the lamentation that shall take place in Israel when the Lord passes in the midst of her. This is followed by a woe upon those who desire the Day of the Lord. We learned in the chapter on Joel the true meaning of this expression—that it has reference to the latter days when the Lord will come in glory to manifest Himself in the destruction of the enemies of righteousness and to exalt those who have loved Him. 

The people of Israel thought they loved the Lord and many of them yearned for the coming of the day of exaltation. Upon these hypocrites Amos pronounced a woe:

Even if the Lord were to come in their day—which He would not-it would be a day of calamity and darkness, not light. 

It would be: 

25 Amos 5:14, 15.

As if a man did flee from a lion, And a bear met him;

And went into the house and leaned his hand on the wall, And a serpent bit him. 

Shall not the day of the Lord be darkness, and not light? 

Even very dark, and no brightness in it? 26

ANOTHER PLEA FOR JUSTICE.-Amos turns again to denounce the false and hypocritical display of religious fervor on the part of the people.27 Of what avail would feasts, solemn assemblies, burnt and meal offerings be in the worship of a righteous God, when their hearts and minds are evil and their actions toward their less fortunate brethren were unjust? All of this outward display was unavailing and Amos cries out for justice in two lines that have become famous:

But let justice well up as waters, And righteousness as a perennial stream.28 

This clarion call to repentance is one of the finest of all times.

WOE UPON THE CARELESS RICH, THE SELF-SAT- ISFIED, AND THE ARROGANT.-Amos next turns his invective on the careless and reckless rich of Israel, on those who are at ease, on the self-satisfied and arrogantin short-on those who, having plenty, take no thought of the sad social and religious state of their country. 

These individuals are absolutely indifferent to the threatened ruin of their people. The prophet indicates that exile is to be their portion, that the nation is to be destroyed because its inhabitants pervert truth and righteousness and trust in their own strength.

Within thirty years what Amos had predicted was fulfilled.

FIVE VISIONS OF THE LORD'S EXECUTION OF 

JUDGMENT. By means of his description of five visions, Amos seeks to impress the importance of what he has previously said upon the people, laying particular emphasis upon the certainty and finality of the judgment. 

These visions are in order a swarm of locusts, devouring fire, the master builder with the plumbline, the basket of summer fruit, and the smitten sanctuary.31 They are effective in making clear that the Lord intends to bring the kingdom of Israel to an end if His people do not repent. Amos held nothing back that the Lord told him to say and did not permit even Amaziah, priest of Bethel and the Archbishop of Canterbury of his day, to intimidate and frighten him from his plain duty. To the contrary, he pointed out that he was no hireling prophet and predicted that Amaziah's wife should play the harlot in the city, that his sons and daughters should fall by the sword, and that he should die in exile in a strange and unclean land,32 

29 The older translations usually render 6:13 as follows: Ye who rejoice in a thing of nought, that say: "Have we not taken to us horns by our own strength?" This does not give good sense because the names of the two cities, Lodebhar (no thing) and Karnaim (horns), have been literally translated. The above rendering brings out the meaning beautifully. 

30 Amos 6:1-8, 11-14. 31 See Amos 7:1-9; 8:1-3; 9:1-6.

JEHOVAH'S LOVE FOR ALL NATIONS.-There are three passages in Amos that clearly portray the prophet's conception that God loves and cares for all nations. 

We have already considered two of them in other connections. The first is found in Amos 1:3-2:3 which, it will be remembered, was a pronouncement of doom upon a number of peoples living near Israel. Jehovah's very pronouncement upon them, however, shows how interested He was in their doings. Failure to live up to the better ideals of living caused Jehovah regret and disappointment. The second is found in Amos 3:2. 

You only have I known of all the families of the earth;

Therefore I will visit upon you all your iniquities. 

This passage as pointed out above, implies that Israel is "chosen" only in the sense that she accepts greater responsibility. The greater the privileges given, the greater the responsibilities. Therefore, God's dealings with all peoples are essentially equal. "God is no respecter of persons," or we may add, of races. 33 

In still another passage Amos makes even clearer the point in question: 

Are ye not as the children of the Ethiopians unto Me, O children of Israel? saith the Lord.

Have not I brought up Israel out of the land of Egypt, And the Philistines from Caphtor, And Aram from Kir?34

Because of her sins Amos considers Israel on the same level as other nations.

THE DAWN OF A BETTER DAY.-Amos has painted to his audience a sorrowful picture of sin and doom. Here 

33 Acts 10:34. 

34 Amos 9:7.

and there to be sure are flashes of hope.35 But mostly it is doom. Suddenly Amos ceases his message of doom and portrays a future of hope and promise. 36 In the latter days the remnant of Israel that has escaped destruction shall be restored to its inheritance, the captivity of its people is to be turned, it shall build cities and plant vineyards, and never be dispossessed.

And I will turn the captivity of My people Israel, And they shall build the waste cities, and inhabit them; 

And they shall plant vineyards, and drink the wine thereof; 

And they shall also make gardens, and eat the fruit of them. 

And I will plant them upon their land, And they shall no more be plucked up

Out of their land which I have given them, Saith the Lord thy God.37

This section, a rosy picture of Israel's future blessed state, has been denied to Amos by many scholars. But the writer can see no reason to doubt its authenticity. 

What would be the point in warning a people if it were completely doomed and without a ray of hope? Furthermore, Amos is in harmony with other prophets who looked forward to Israel's future blessings.

So far as Israel, as a kingdom and a people, is sinful, it is to be destroyed from off the face of the earth. But there is always a divine kernel in the nation, by virtue of its divine election, a holy seed out of which the Lord will form a new and holy people and kingdom of God.38

CHAPTER V OBADIAH-PROPHET OF EDOM'S DOOM 

HE PROPHET AND HIS BOOK.-Obadiah is an other prophet of whose life and ministry we know little or nothing beyond what can be deduced from the twenty-one verses of his prophecy. 

His book is not only the smallest among the prophets, but also it is the smallest in the Old Testament. But as the English scholar Robertson Smith pointed out many years ago, its difficulty is "out of all proportion to its length." Various writers have attempted-unsuccessfully, we believe to identify Obadiah with one of the dozen or more men of the same name mentioned in the Old Testament.¹ Obadiah's name, judging from the way it is vocalized in our present Hebrew Old Testament, signifies "worshipper of Jehovah". Some Greek manuscripts of the 

Old Testament presuppose the meaning "servant of Jehovah".

The caption of the prophecy, The vision of Obadiah, implies to the author the fact that Obadiah was a seera seer in the sense that he saw the future with supernatural objectivity as distinguished from his own thinking and imagining. He was what the Hebrews called a chozeh or gazer. That Obadiah saw the salvation of Israel and other important events in the latter days may be in part responsible for the preservation of his little book.

The questions relative to the date and unity of the Book of Obadiah have proved puzzling and perplexing to scholars. Very competent authorities have differed 

1See, e. g., I Kgs. 18:3-16; II Chroni. 17:7; 25:7; 34:12.

by as much as nearly six hundred years-from 889 B. C. to 312 B. C.-in the matter of dating. Some hold the prophecy to be a unity, contending that Obadiah wrote all of it; others say that the prophet wrote only part of it and that additional verses were later appended by unknown authors. I shall not confuse and bewilder the reader with a technical discussion of these points other than to say that I see no good reason to doubt the unity of Obadiah. As to the date of the book-let me assign it to approximately 730 B. C. I do this with an open mind, feeling that the last word has not been said on the subject.

One very interesting literary feature of Obadiah should be called to the reader's attention. All students of the subject have noted very marked resemblances between Obadiah 

1-9 and Jeremiah 49:7-22. Anyone who will take the trouble to compare them will be convinced, I am sure, that these resemblances are not purely coincidental. They are usually explained in one of three ways: 1. Jeremiah borrowed from Obadiah, 2. Obadiah borrowed from 

Jeremiah, or, 3. Both borrowed from a common original. My own feeling is that Obadiah is the older document, though in a few places Jeremiah differs in such a way as to lead one to believe his version is original. 

This suggests a fourth solution, namely, that both saw or had revealed to them the same things and expressed them in somewhat differing thought patterns. We find this kind of phenomenon occurring very often in the 

Doctrine and Covenants, where the prophet Joseph Smith makes obvious allusions to certain Biblical passages, but not always in precisely their same language. The reader will recall some examples quoted in the chapter on Joel. 

A thorough discussion of this question would lead us into

the problem of the nature of inspiration, and we desist. 

The prophecy of Obadiah may for convenience be studied in four divisions: 1. Announcement of the doom of Edom (1-9), 2. The cause of Edom's doom (10-14), 3. The Day of the Lord and retribution upon the nations (15, 16), 4. The exaltation of Israel and the coming of saviors on Mount Zion (17-21). 

Before treating these divisions something should be said about Edom and its relations with Israel. A better understanding of the spirit of Obadiah's prophecy will be gained thereby.

THE TRADITIONAL RELATIONS BETWEEN ISRAEL 

AND EDOM.-The history of the relations between Israel and Edom is from the beginning fraught with envy and hate. In Genesis 36:1 we have the following statement: "Now these are the generations of Esau-the same is Edom." This recalls to us the struggle for supremacy from birth, or even before, of Esau and his younger brother Jacob (Israel). According to the story in Genesis: 

Isaac entreated the Lord for his wife, because she was barren; and the Lord let Himself be entreated of him, and Rebekah his wife conceived. And the children struggled together within her; and she said: "If it be so, wherefore do I live?" And she went to inquire of the Lord. And the Lord said unto her: 

Two nations are in thy womb, And two peoples shall be separated from thy bowels;

And the one people shall be stronger than the other people; 

And the elder shall serve the younger.2

The sequel of this story is familiar to us all-the eventual triumph of Jacob over his brother in accordance with the revelation of the Lord to Rebekah. Esau loses his birthright to his brother for a mess of pottage and 

2Gen. 25:21-23.

finally the holy patriarchal inheritance also. Esau, it will be recalled, married among the Canaanites, which was a great trial to his parents. He even threatened the life of 

Jacob who was forced to flee to Haran, the home of his kinsmen. In these stories the Old Testament writers strongly contrast the two brothers. Jacob, the younger brother and progenitor of the tribes of Israel, seeks to serve God and obtain His blessings at any cost. On the other hand-to put it in the words of Sir George Adam Smith:

Esau is a profane person; with no conscience of a birthright, no faith in the future, no capacity for vision; dead to the unseen, the clamoring only for the satisfaction of his appetites.

The same was probably the character of his descendants; who had, of course, their own gods, like every other people in that Semitic world, but were essentially irreligious, living for food, spoil and vengeance-a people who deserved even more than the Philistines to have their names descend as a symbol of hardness and obscurantism. It is no contradiction to this that the one intellectual quality imputed to the Edmonites should be that of shrewdness and a wisdom obviously worldly. The wise men of Edom, the cleverness of Mount Esau were notorious. It is the race which has given to history only the Herodsclever, scheming, ruthless statesmen, as able as false and bitter, as shrewd in policy as destitute of ideals. That fox, cried Christ, and crying stamped the race. 

Israel's other enemies such as the Canaanites, Philistines, Assyrians, Babylonians, Persians, Greeks, Egyptians, and Syrians rose and fell in successive waves, but the Edomites, like the poor, were always with them. Hidden 

3For a fuller account the reader is referred to Sidney B. Sperry, The 

Spirit of the Old Testament, chap. 4. 

4Heb. 12:16. 

5Obad. 8. "George A. Smith, The Book of the Twelve Prophets (New and Rev. 

Ed.), II, 182f.

in the mountain-lands of Seir, southeast of Palestine and extending even into the desert, the Edomite nation was ever a threatening menace. Among magnificent and scarcely penetrable rocky defiles and with good water and food supplies, its people usually had little trouble in defending themselves. Hence the cry of Obadiah: 

O thou that dwellest in the clefts of the rock, Thy habitation on high; 

That sayest in thy heart: "Who shall bring me down to the ground?"

The self-sufficiency of the Edomites was aggravated by their key position on several important trade routes in ancient times. In early times they became traders and middlemen and many of them became wealthy. We understand readily, therefore, when Obadiah cries out: 

How is Esau searched out! 

How are his hidden treasures sought out!

Israelite kings who wanted the trade from the East were compelled to conquer Edom in order to control it. 

Hence revolts were frequent and after each one the hatred of the contending parties only increased. Through the centuries the Edomites became more and more in the eyes of the prophets of Israel a symbol of wickedness. Edom represented the world with all of its crass materialism and lack of spiritual vision or feeling. It is interesting to note that in later times the Jewish scribes applied the name of Edom to Rome.⁹

THE INTERPRETATION OF OBADIAH.-In interpret ing the prophecy of Obadiah one ought, first of all, to 

7Obad. 8. 

8Obad. 6. 

9Smith, op. cit., II, 185.

read it through several times, using a good modern translation. In the light of what has already been said about the relations of Edom and Israel the reader will readily understand why, in the opening verses (1-9), Edom is the target of a withering prediction of doom from the Lord. She is to be made small among the nations and greatly despised; her arrogance is fatal, for she shall be brought low; she is to be completely cleared out by her enemies worse than if she were visited by ordinary thieves or robbers, for they would leave a few things; in the midst of her calamity even her allies will forsake and abandon her to her enemies so as not to be entangled in her troubles; finally, the worldly wisdom and insight of her wise men will prove of no avail against the slaughter to come.

THE VISION OF OBADIAH 

Thus saith the Lord God concerning Edom:

We have heard a message from the Lord, And an ambassador is sent among the nations: "Arise ye, and let us rise up against her in battle.” 

Behold, I make thee small among the nations; 

Thou are greatly despised. 

The pride of thy heart hath beguiled thee,

O thou that dwellest in the clefts of the rock, Thy habitation on high; 

That sayest in thy heart: "Who shall bring me down to the ground?" 

Though thou make thy nest as high as the eagle, And though thou set it among the stars, I will bring thee down from thence, saith the Lord. 

If thieves come to thee, if robbers by night- 

How art thou cut off!- 

Would they not steal till they had enough? 

If grape-gatherers come to thee, Would they not leave some gleaming grapes?

How is Esau searched out? 

How are his hidden treasures sought out? 

All the men of thy confederacy 

Have conducted thee to the border;

The men that were at peace with thee Have beguiled thee, and prevailed against thee; 

They that eat thy bread lay a snare under thee,

In whom there is no discernment. 

Shall I not in that day, saith the Lord,

Destroy the wise men out of Edom, And discernment out of the mount of Esau? 

And thy mighty men, O Teman,10 shall be dismayed,

To the end that everyone may be cut off from the mount of Esau by slaughter.11 

In verses 10-14 the Lord indicates the reasons for 

Edom's doom. These may be summed up as follows: 

1. Violence done to Jacob (Israel), 2. They gloat over the distress of Israel, and instead of aiding her actually give support to her enemies. In verses 12-14, the effect is given of a speaker actually face to face with the situation and giving appropriate advice to the Edomites. 

For the violence done to thy brother Jacob shame shall cover thee, And thou shalt be cut off forever. 

In the day of thy standing aloof, In the day of strangers carrying away his substance, And foreigners entered his gates, And cast lots upon Jerusalem,12 

Even thou wast as one of them.

In these verses the author has followed the Hebrew rather literally. In relation to this section Professor Keil stated it well when he said:

Wrong, or violence, is all the more reprehensible, when it is committed against a brother. The fraternal relation in which 

Edom stood towards Judah is still more sharply defined by the name Jacob, since Esau and Jacob were twin brothers. The consciousness that the Israelites were their brethren, ought to have impelled the Edmonites to render helpful support to the oppressed Judeans. Instead of this, they not only revelled with scornful and malignant pleasure in the misfortune of the brother nation, but endeavored to increase it still further by rendering active support to the enemy.15

As one studies and ponders over the two sections of Obadiah thus far considered, he acquires a feeling that 13That is, do not make the mouth wide in a gesture of contempt and ridicule. 

14Obad. 10-14. 

15C. F. Keil and F. Delitzsch, Biblical Commentary on the Old Testa ment (The Twelve Minor Prophets), I, 360.

the prophet has a message to communicate far deeper than the destruction of the Edomite nation. To be sure, the destruction is one of his points, but he also used Edom in a symbolic sense. One begins to sense this in the very first verse of the prophecy: 

We have heard a message from the Lord, And an ambassador is sent among the nations; "Arise ye, and let us rise up against her in battle."

Edom is viewed here as the object of the fury of any or all nations without discrimination. The language used seems too strong to be taken literally. It appears very probable, therefore, that Edom is used as a symbol of the wicked world with all its pomp and power, the devastation of which is forecast in the verses following. 

All nations, regardless of their worldly wisdom, shall fall if they direct their wrath and power against God's people. 

That this interpretation of Obadiah is correct is made certain, I believe, by the next section, verses 15, 16.

For the day of the Lord is near upon all the nations; 

As thou hast done, it shall be done unto thee; 

Thy dealing shall return upon thine own head. 

For as ye have drunk upon My holy mountain, So shall all the nations drink continually, And they shall drink, and swallow down, And shall be as though they had not been.

It will be remembered that in our study of Joel we found the meaning of the Day of the Lord. It refers, according to the Doctrine and Covenants, to a time yet future in which the coming of the Son of Man shall take place.16 At that time, the power, glory, and judgment of the Lord shall be manifested unto the nations.17 In 

16 See D. & C. 25:39 and consult the chapter on Joel. 

17D. & C. 45:44.

the verses just quoted we find Obadiah speaking of the Day of the Lord and of God's judgments upon the nations in essentially the same terms as we found in Joel. 

When Obadiah says, "As thou hast done, it shall be done unto thee," he is, therefore, referring to Edom as a symbol of the ungodly world of the latter days. In the 

Doctrine and Covenants the Lord makes a most interesting reference to Edom under such symbolism. After speaking of the difficulties in the latter days, when peace shall be taken from the earth and the devil shall have power over his own dominion, He continues: 

And also the Lord shall have power over his saints, and shall reign in their midst, and shall come down in judgment upon Idumea, or the world (italics ours).18 

From Maccabean times (167 B. C.) Edom was known as Idumea. Hence the allusion is easily understood in the light of history.

In the last section of Obadiah, verses 17-21, the prophet sees the eventual restoration of Israel. The picture is essentially that which we have already seen in the prophecies thus far studied-Hosea, Joel, and Amos. I believe that Obadiah saw clearly the blessings of God upon the various divisions of Israel in their different geographical locations. Mount Zion is particularly pointed out. This, as we have learned, refers to America and especially the city of the New Jerusalem, which is to be a holy place.19 Latter-day Saints know that this continent is especially reserved for the descendants of Joseph

The last verse of Obadiah relative to "saviors" on Mount Zion has always been much quoted by the Mormon people and interpreted in a special sense. It will be appropriate to discuss it at this point. 

On Sunday, January 21, 1844, the prophet Joseph

Smith preached a sermon at the southeast corner of the Nauvoo Temple to several thousand people on the subject of the sealing power of the priesthood. In his discourse, a synopsis of which was reported by Elder Wilford Woodruff, he says: 

The Bible says, "I will send you Elijah the Prophet before the coming of the great and dreadful day of the Lord; and he shall turn the hearts of the fathers to the children, and the hearts of the children to the fathers, lest I come and smite the earth with a curse.' 9924 

And the word turn here should be translated bind, or seal.

But what is the object of this important mission? or how is it to be fulfilled? The keys are to be delivered, the spirit of Elijah is to come, the Gospel to be established, the Saints of God gathered, Zion built up, and the Saints to come up as saviors on 

Mount Zion (last italics ours).

But how are they to become saviors on Mount Zion? (Italics ours.) By building their temples, erecting their baptismal fonts, and going forth and receiving all the ordinances, baptisms, confirmations, washings, anointings, ordinations and sealing powers upon their heads, in behalf of all their progenitors who are dead, and redeem them that they may come forth in the first resurrection and be exalted to thrones of glory with them; and herein is the chain that binds the hearts of the fathers to the children, and the children to the fathers, which fulfills the mission of Elijah. And I would to God that this temple was now done, that we might go into it, and go to work and improve our time, and make use of the seals while they are on earth.2

These words seem to imply that the modern Ameri-
