CHAPTER VIII NAHUM-PROPHET OF NINEVEH'S DOOM 

'HE PROPHET'S NAME AND HOME.-The prophet's name occurs nowhere else in the Bible except in the genealogy of Joseph the husband of Mary the mother of Christ. It means "Comforter" or "Consoler." The superscription of the Book of Nahum refers to the prophet as the "Elkoshite." This latter designation probably has reference to the place of his birth, rather than to his ancestor's name. It seems to mean that 

Nahum came from a place called Elkosh, in the same sense that Micah the Morashtite came from Moresheth.³

The Septuagint refers to the prophet as "Nahum the 

Elkesite." Unfortunately, we do not know the exact location of Elkosh, which is not mentioned elsewhere in the Bible. Several sites have been identified as the place. 

1. The modern Al-Kush, a small town about twenty-six miles north of Nineveh. Here a small "plaster box" has been pointed out by Christians, Jews, and Mohammedans alike as Nahum's tomb. This identification has little to recommend it, and the tradition in its favor can be traced back only to the sixteenth century A. D. 2. Ain-Japhata, a town south of Babylon, where in 1165 A. D. Nahum's grave was pointed out to a Jewish traveler, Benjamin of 

Tudela. In his "Itinerary" the Rabbi says: "To Ain 

Japhata, three parasangs; this place contains the sepulchre of the prophet Nahum who rests in peace." The site has 

1See Luke 3:25. 2Nahum 1:1. 3 Micah 1:1. 

4The Greek Version of the Old Testament. 

5See Asher, The Itinerary of Rabbi Benjamin of Tudela, I, 110. Page 

68.2 of Hebrew text.

nothing more to commend it than the statement given. 

3. St. Jerome, great Bible translator (circa 400 A. D.). located Elkosh in Galilee. He said:

Elkesie (or Helkesei) is still at this day a hamlet in Galilee, small indeed, and scarcely showing traces by ruins of ancient buildings, but for all that known to the Jews and pointed out to me by a guide. Most scholars reject this identification because the prophet's book gives no sign that he had any connection with Galilee. 4. Certain scholars have identified Elkosh with Capernaum of New Testament fame. Capernaum means "village of Nahum" and it is supposed that Elkosh was changed to that name because of the prophet's fame. 

Aside from the name itself there is no support for this site. 5. Another tradition places Elkosh in southern Judah.

A Syriac account of the prophets falsely attributed to 

Epiphanius, bishop of Salamis on the Island of Cyprus during the latter part of the forth century A. D., speaks of Nahum as "of Elkosh beyond Bet Gabre, of the tribe of Simeon." This site probably corresponds with the modern Beit Jibrin through which the writer has traveled many times by foot or by car. Since a place in Judah seems to be more in harmony with the prophet's interest, many authorities are inclined at the present time to place 

Elkosh in southern Palestine.

THE DATE AND TIMES OF THE PROPHET.—The date of Nahum's activities has to be deduced from certain statements made in the prophecy. In chapter 3:8-10 reference is made to the destruction of the city No Amon, the Egyptian Thebes, as an already accomplished fact. We know Thebes was captured by Assurbanipal, the Assyrian, 6Quoted in F. C. Eiselen, The Prophetic Books of the Old Testament, 

II, 504,

in 663 B. C. Therefore, Nahum's prophecy must have been written after that date. And since Nahum's prophecy deals with the coming destruction of Nineveh, we know it must have been written before 612 B. C., the date of her downfall. We may date Nahum's ministry with some degree of probability, therefore, between the years 663 

B. C. and 612 B. C.

Because Nahum deals almost exclusively with Assyria's or rather Nineveh's downfall, it may be of value to review Israel's relations with this cruel colossus of the north. In the year 853 B. C. Shalmaneser III, king of

Assyria, defeated (so he says) a coalition of kings including Ahab, king of Israel. This was the first occasion on which Israel came into definite contact with her northern neighbor, but it was not to be the last. In 841 B. C., Jehu, king of Israel, paid tribute to Shalmaneser III. And in 

738 B. C. Menahem of Israel was forced to pay tribute to Tiglath-Pileser III. Tiglath-Pileser in the year 732

B. C. replaced Pekah, the Israelite king, Hoshea, but northern Israel suffered through the Assyrian expedition and a large part of the population was deported to Assyria. 

King Ahaz of Judah also submitted to the Assyrian yoke, and Assyrian religious customs were introduced by 721 B. C. The kingdom of Israel came to an end when the capital city of Samaria capitulated after a siege conducted by Shalmaneser V and Sargon II. Sargon also captured 

Ashdod in southwestern Palestine in 711 B. C. Then in 710 B. C. followed the great Assyrian expedition against Judah. The suffering caused by the cruel Assyrians was terrible. Since we have Sennacherib's "Annals," anyone may read the Assyrian king's own account." There followed the invasion of Judah by Esarhaddon about 

See D. D. Luckenbill, The Annals of Sennacherib.

679 B. C., if the account in II Chronicles 33:10, 11 can be trusted. Manasseh, king of Judah, was carried captive to Nineveh and was forced to pay homage to Esarhaddon. 

Ezra 4:2 speaks also of settlements made in Palestine by 

Esarhaddon, and Ezra 4:10 of immigrants introduced by 

Assurbanipal mentioned above. This brings us down to approximately Nahum's own times. Dr. George L. Robinson speaks of Assurbanipal the Assyrian king who destroyed No Amon or Thebes, in the following terms:

Assurbanipal was exceptionally cruel. He even boasts of his violence and shameful atrocities: how he ruthlessly tore off the lips and limbs of kings, forced three captured rulers of Elam to drag his chariot through the streets, compelled a prince to wear round his neck the decapitated head of his king, and how he and his queen feasted in a garden with the head of a Chaldean monarch whom he had forced to commit suicide hanging from a tree above them. No other king of Assyria ever boasts of such inhuman and atrocious barbarities. As he advanced toward Egypt on one of his expeditions, twenty-two kings are said to have paid him homage; upon his arrival both 

Memphis, the capital of Lower Egypt, and Thebes, the capital of Upper Egypt, were successfully wrested from Tirhakah and cruelly punished. The poor people of Judah and Jerusalem were spectators of all these horrors. Indeed they had beheld for generations an almost endless succession of Assyrian invasions of Palestine . . . and now Assurbanipal; the worst seemed yet to come. Nahum and his compatriots in Jerusalem apparently lay bound and helpless in the grasp of a cruel, tyrannical enemy. 

So far as the ordinary man could see the power of Assyria was still strong after nearly two hundred years. 

To be sure, there was a revolt about 650 B. C. in Assyria, but Nineveh, the capital city, was not endangered. But after 626 B. C., the year of Assurbanipal's death, Assyria showed some signs of cracking up. The prophet Nahum seems to have foreseen the imminent fall of Nineveh, and 

8G. L. Robinson, The Twelve Minor Prophets, pp. 108ff.

we may date his prophecy about 620 B. C. with the possibility of an error either way of several years. If persons reading the prophecy are shocked at Nahum's predictions of terrible woe and desolation upon Nineveh, let them keep in mind the history of Assyria's cruelty. 

A man is no less a true prophet because he denounces and predicts the downfall of a power as cruel for its time as that led today by Hitler and his companions in international banditry. The author believes that Sir George 

Adam Smith is too severe upon Nahum, of whose writings we have but forty-seven verses, when he declares:

His heart, for all its bigness, holds room only for the bitter memories, the baffled hopes, the unappeased hatreds of a hundred years . . . he represents no single movement of his people's progress, but the passion of the whole epoch drawing to a close. 

Nahum's book is one great At Last!

And, therefore, while Nahum is a worse prophet than Zephaniah, with less conscience and insight, he is a greater poet . . We protest against such a statement because common experience in dealing with people ought to teach us that few public men can be righteously judged on the basis of a single speech even several times the length of Nahum's prophecy. If the prophet Joseph Smith were judged only by the first section of the Doctrine and Covenants which deals with God's threatened judgments on the present generation, we might say that he was narrow, pessimistic, and a poor prophet. Such statements would not be at all true, because we know from the prophet's other writings and from statements of his contemporaries, that he was generous, lovable, broadminded, and tolerant to a fault. 

9G. A. Smith, The Book of the Twelve Prophets (New and Rev. Ed.), II, 89f.

Such may have been true also of Nahum. Statements of the character and teaching of Old Testament prophets must be made carefully, especially when we have but few of their writings.

From what we have said of Nahum it is apparent that he could have been a contemporary of Jeremiah and 

Zephaniah and possibly acquainted with them. But of this latter we cannot, of course, be certain. 

THE CONTENT AND STYLE OF THE BOOK.-The 

Book of Nahum in both its Hebrew and English dress consists of forty-seven verses divided into three chapters. 

The first two chapters in the Hebrew Version each contain fourteen verses, whereas the corresponding chapters in the 

Engish translation contain fifteen and fourteen verses respectively. The text of Nahum has been fairly well preserved with the possible exception of chapters 1:2-2:2, which is supposed by some scholars to have originally formed an alphabetic acrostic.10 I am dubious that an acrostic was ever intended, and even then the text of the passage may be considered only in fair shape. It has been supposed by certain authorities that chapter one of Nahum is late and was added by some editor after the Exile. The question is bound up with that of the presence of an acrostic. I consider it far safer to assume that Nahum was the original author of chapter one than to rely on mere theories, at present incapable of proof, that he was not. For our purpose, therefore, in this book we shall consider that Nahum was the author of practically all the text that goes under his name.

The prophecy of Nahum, as we have pointed out before, centers about one theme-the destruction of Nin- 10 The lines or stanzas of an alphabetic acrostic begin with the letters of the alphabet in regular order.

eveh. The three chapters respectively deal with the decree of Ninevah's destruction, the siege and capture of the city, her wickedness, and inevitable doom. The book may conveniently be studied according to the following outline.

I. 

Nineveh's doom decreed (1:2-15). 

A. The Lord's avenging wrath (1:2-10).

B. Reproaches or threats against a foe (presumably 

Nineveh) and words of comfort and deliverance to

Judah (1:11-15). 

II. The siege and destruction of Nineveh (2:1-13).

A. The attack upon Nineveh and sack of the city (2:1-10). 

B.

Sarcastic exultation over the destruction of the city (2:11-13). 

III. Nineveh's wickedness and inevitable doom (3:1-19).

A. Retribution upon the city for outrages against other nations (3:1-7). 

B. Nineveh's fate to be that of No Amon in Egypt 

C. Nineveh's inability to save herself (3:12-19).

Nahum was a master of Hebrew style. The poetry of his prophecy is among the finest in the Old Testament. 

None of the minor prophets can be said to be finer in this regard, and only Isaiah among the major prophets possibly excels him in animation, boldness and sublimity; or, in proportion to the size of his book, in the freshness, richness, variety, elegance, and force of his imagery. "His language is strong and brilliant; his rhythm rumbles and rolls, leaps and flashes, like the horses and chariots he describes."11

Nahum is especially marked for his descriptive pow- 

11Smith, op. cit., II, 90.

ers. He is vivid and impetuous, fiery and vigorous, colorful and dramatic, and notable for his picturesque phraseology. Sir George Adam Smith declares the Book of Nahum is composed of three odes. The first, chapter 

1, "is general and theological, affirming God's power of vengeance and the certainty of the overthrow of His enemies. The second, Ch. 2., with the omission of ver. 2, and the third, Ch.. 3, can hardly be disjoined; they both present a vivid picture of the siege, the storm and the spoiling of Nineveh."12

In chapter 2:11-13 there is a beautiful allegory in which Nahum, with triumphant sarcasm, asks where the residence is of the once victorious and rapacious foe.

THE SUPERSCRIPTION.-The superscription of the 

Book of Nahum is composed of two parts and reads as follows: 

The burden of Nineveh. The book of the vision of Nahum the Elkoshite.13

Nahum is the first of the prophetic books to begin with the announcement that it is a massa or burden as indeed it is the first and only one to state that it is a book. The Hebrew word massa is derived from a root which means to "lift up," "to carry," "to heave." Consequently, the noun means "a carrying," "lifting," or "burden." But such a definition thus applied to a prophecy is strange to the average reader and devoid of meaning. 

Nor is the meaning entirely clear even to scholars. St. 

Jerome,14 centuries ago in commenting on Habakkuk 1:1 where the same word occurs, observes that "Massa never 

12Smith, op. cit., II, 77. 

13 Nahum 1:1. 

14The great Bible translator (400 A. D.) who rendered much of the scripture into Latin.

occurs in the title, except when it is evidently grave and full of weight and labor." Many scholars have approved this statement and point out that burden is never placed before simple announcements of salvation, but only before prophecies of a threatening nature. Other scholars translate massa as utterance or oracle. 

In the second part of the superscription the word vision indicates that Nahum's book is the book of seeing, i. e., what the prophet saw through the spirit concerning the coming destruction of Nineveh. Nahum was, therefore, a chozeh or seer.

THE LORD'S AVENGING WRATH.-The prophecy opens with a sublime description of the attributes and works of God. This is done for the purpose of inspiring the Jews to have confidence in Him as their protector.

Stated in the words of Dr. E. Henderson, an English scholar of nearly a century ago

Nothing can exceed in grandeur and sublimity the description which the prophet furnishes of the Divine character. The attributes of infinite purity, inflexible rectitude, irresistible power, and boundless goodness, set forth and illustrated by images borrowed from the history of the Hebrews, the scenery of Palestine, and the more astounding phenomena of nature, present to view a God worthy of the profoundest reverence, the most unbounded confidence, and the most intensive love.15

The Lord is shown to be a God of vengeance and mercy. These two aspects of the Lord's character are, of course, not contradictory. When men boast in their own strength and set at naught the counsels of God and follow after the dictates of their own will and carnal desires, they must fall and incur the vengeance of a just God upon 

15E. Henderson, The Book of the Twelve Minor Prophets, 2nd Ed., p. 273.

them.16 The Assyrians were overwhelmingly guilty and ripe for God's vengeance.

Nahum was amply justified in announcing those facts. The prophet points out also that the Lord is faithful to those who rely on Him and that darkness shall pursue His enemies. He assures Judah that God will bring utter destruction upon the ancient foe. 

A jealous God and vengeful is Jehovah; 

Jehovah is vengeful and full of wrath;

Jehovah is vengeful toward His enemies, And He is implacable to His foes. 

Jehovah is slow to anger, and great 

And will certainly not absolve. in power, Jehovah, His way is in storm and whirlwind, And the clouds are the dust of His feet.

He curbs the sea, and makes it dry, And all the streams He dries up: 

Bashan is withered and Carmel, And the flower of Lebanon is withered. 

Mountains quake before him, And the hills melt; 

Earth heaves at His presence,

Yea, the world, and all who dwell in it. 

Before His indignation, who can stand? 

And who can abide in the fierceness of His anger? 

His fury is poured out like fire, And the rocks are rent by Him.

Good is Jehovah for a stronghold in the day of trouble; 

And He knows those who trust Him.

But with an overwhelming flood He makes an utter end among 

His adversaries, And His enemies He pursues into darkness. 

What think ye concerning Jehovah? 

He is one who makes an utter destruction: 

Trouble does not rise up the second time.

For though they be like tangled thorns, 16 See D. & C. 3:4.

And as soaked with their drink,

They will be consumed completely like dry stubble.¹ 

NINEVEH REPROACHED AND JUDAH COMFORTED. 

-In the remaining verses of chapter one the Lord reproaches or threatens a foe which is in all probability Nineveh. The latter's doom is decreed because she has devised evil against the Lord. Judah's yoke, on the other hand, is to be broken and her bonds burst asunder. The gods of Nineveh are to be rooted up, and Judah is promised that the "wicked one" shall no more pass through her. In verse fifteen the prophet quotes words apparently from Isaiah 52:7.

Behold upon the mountains the feet of him 

That bringeth good tidings, that publisheth peace!

These words are referred to by the Savior¹8 as recorded in the Book of Mormon and in the Doctrine and Covenants. 19 They seemingly deal with the principle of revelation to Israel in the latter days. Nahum uses them, however, in dealing with the situation at hand. The word of the Lord through him is to the effect that Judah's religious feasts, otherwise interrupted by the Assyrians, are to be kept. Furthermore, the Jews shall never again be menaced by their enemy. 

THE SIEGE AND SACK OF NINEVEH. Having prophetically announced the overthrow of the Assyrian power, Nahum proceeds to describe, as if truly present, the siege and capture of Nineveh. This is done with graphic minuteness, and in the most sublime and vivid manner. 17Nahum 1:2-10. 

18 See II Nephi 20:39, 40. 

19D. & C. 113:10.

We have already presented a brief description of the city of Nineveh in the chapter on Jonah, to which the reader is referred. 20 There only remains to be said a word about the circumstances of Nineveh's fall so as to give a little more background for Nahum's prophetic poetry. 

After the death of Assurbanipal, king of Assyria, in 626 B. C., Babylon recovered her independence under the 

Chaldean, Nabopolassor. Little is said in the records of the latter until the year 616 B. C., when the vengeance of oppressed peoples began to assert itself very strongly against Assyria. In that year Nabopolassor marched north and inflicted a severe defeat on the Assyrians at a place called Kablini. He failed to hold his ground here as indeed he did again in another campaign up the valley of the Tigris. However, he managed to return safely to 

Babylon. Early in the year 614 B. C. the Medes also appeared on the scene and captured Ashur, while the Chaldean army hastened northward to join them. When junction was made by the two armies, Nabopolassor and Kyaxares, king of the Medes, made an alliance. In the year 612 B. C. the combined forces made an assault on 

Nineveh. The fighting in the suburbs and immediately before the walls was most desperate, and three terrific engagements took place before the defenses could be stormed. Finally, however, the allies' superior strength won out and Nineveh fell. It is with these great engagements that the following poetry of Nahum deals. 

The hammer is come up before thee; 

See the rampart, keep watch on the way, Strengthen the loins, fortify thy power mightily.

For Jehovah causes the pride of Jacob to return, Like the pride of Israel! 

20 See pp. 99-100.

For the plunderers plundered them, And destroyed their vine branches. 

The shields of his heroes are red, The warriors are in scarlet:

The chariots flash with steel in the day of his preparation, And the horsemen are prancing.

The chariots rage through the streets, They dash around in the broad ways:

The appearance of them is like torches, Like lightnings they dart to and fro.21

He calls to his nobles: they stumble in their march; 

They hasten to the wall, and the bulwark is prepared. 

The river gates are opened, and the palace is dissolved. 

And it is decreed; she is uncovered, she is carried away, And her handmaids mourn as with the voice of doves, Beating upon their breasts. 

And Nineveh! like a reservoir of waters are her waters, And they flee away; Stand, stand (they cry); but no one rallies them. 

Plunder the silver, plunder the gold:

For there is no end of the treasure, a mass of all precious things. 

She is empty, and void, and waste: 

And the heart melts, and the knees shake. 

And anguish is in all loins, And all the faces lose their color.22 

A SARCASTIC ALLEGORY OVER NINEVEH'S DE- 

STRUCTION. Having described the sack of Nineveh, Nahum proceeds to exult over the wicked city. The exultation takes the form of a beautiful allegory. In the last verse the literal is intermixed with the figurative. 

Where is the lions' den, And the young lions' feeding place, Where the lion and the lioness walked, The lion's whelp, and none made them afraid? 

21Some persons have interpreted this verse as having reference to lighted automobiles and trains in the latter days. It is obvious that the context and purpose of Nahum's prophecy permit no such interpretation. 22Nahum 2:1-10.

The lion tore enough for his whelps, And strangled for his lionesses, And filled his pits with prey, And his dens with rapine.

Behold, I am against thee (it is the oracle of Jehovah of hosts), And I will consume her chariots in the smoke, And the sword will devour thy young lions: 

And I will cut off thy prey from the earth, And the voice of thy envoys will no more be heard.23

RETRIBUTION UPON NINEVEH FOR PAST OUT- RAGES AGAINST OTHER NATIONS.-In the first verse of chapter three the prophet again turns on Nineveh and pronounces a woe upon the bloody city. Then follows a vivid description of the noise of the conflict and the glitter of the paraphernalia of war. Nahum points out that the judgment on the city is well merited. She has acted the part of a harlot, and the harlot's fate she must endure. No one shall have pity upon her or comfort her in her hour of destruction. 

Woe to the city of blood! 

And full of guile and robbery, ceaselessly preying. 

Hark! The cracking of the whip, And hark! the rumbling of the wheels; 

And horses prancing; 

And the chariots rattling; 

Cavalry charging, and the flash of swords, And the lightning of lances; 

And a mass of slain, and a weight of corpses, And endless dead bodies- 

They stumble on their dead Because of the multitude of the harlotries of the harlot, The well-favored mistress of witchcrafts, She who sold nations with her harlotries, And families with her witchcrafts. 23Nahum 2:11-13.

Behold, I am against thee (it is the oracle of Jehovah of hosts), And I will uncover the skirts to thy face; 

And I will show nations thy nakedness, And kingdoms thy shame. 

And I will cast filth upon thee, and disgrace thee, And will set thee as a gazing-stock; 

So that every one who sees thee will shrink from thee, and say “Nineveh is laid waste; who will pity her? 

Whence shall I seek comforters for thee?"24

NINEVEH'S FATE TO BE THAT OF THE EGYPTIAN CITY OF NO AMON.-Nineveh, the prophet observes, is no better than the Egyptian city of No Amon (Thebes), and her great strength and natural resources will be of no more avail. Even as Assurbanipal had devastated the great Egyptian city, the remnants of whose greatness are described by Dr. James H. Breasted as "the mightiest ruins of ancient civilization to be found anywhere in the world," so should Nineveh be despoiled and destroyed. 

Art thou better than No-amon,

Which sat upon the Nile streams-waters round about her— 

Whose rampart was the sea, and of the sea her wall?

Ethiopia and Egypt were her strength, and it was without end; 

Put and Libya were among thy helpers.

Even she was for exile; she went into captivity: 

Her children likewise were dashed in pieces at the corners of all the streets: 

And they cast lots for her nobles, And all her great men were bound in chains. 

Thou also wilt be drunken, thou wilt grow faint. 

Thou also wilt seek a defence against the enemy.2 25 

NINEVEH'S INABILITY TO SAVE HERSELF.-Nahum last of all proceeds to describe the city's great fortresses as ripe figs ready to be shaken into the mouth of the eater. 24Nahum 3:1-7. 

25 Nahum 3:8-11.

Her people are weak as women and unable to prevent the entrance of the enemy into the city. The siege is near at hand, preparation should be made for it, but all resistance will be in vain for the sword will cut off all the inhabitants. There is no healing for the hurt of the city, and all who hear the news about her will clap their hands. 

All thy fortresses are like fig trees with the firstripe figs: 

If they be shaken, they fall into the mouth of the eater. 

Behold, thy people in the midst of thee are women; 

The gates of thy land will certainly be opened to thy enemies: 

Fire devours thy bolts. 

Draw thee water for the siege, strengthen thy fortresses: 

Go into mud and tread the clay,

Lay hold of the brick-mould. 

There the fire devours thee; the sword cuts thee off, It devours thee like the locust: 

Make thyself many like the grasshoppers. 

Thou hast multiplied thy merchants above the stars of heaven. 

The locust spreads itself and flies away. 

Thy crowned ones are like locusts, and thy commanders like the swarms of grasshoppers,

Which encamp on the walls in the cold day, But when the sun has risen they vanish, And one does not know the place where they are. 

Thy shepherds slumber, O king of Assyria: 

Thy nobles are at rest: 

Thy people are scattered on the mountains, And no one gathers them. 

There is no healing for thy hurt; 

Thy wound is fatal. 

All who hear the news of thee will clap the hands at thee; 

For upon them has not thy wickedness passed continually?26

THE TEACHING OF NAHUM.-Many reflections of value may pass through the mind as one reads the Book of Nahum, but there is very little direct religious teach- 

26 Nahum 3:12-19.

ing therein. Most of what there is is contained in chapter one. There one finds emphasized divine holiness, vengeance, and mercy. The manifestation of God's vengeance results in the destruction of the wicked, and His demonstration of mercy in the salvation of the oppressed. Faith in the Lord will secure for men his favor and protection. 

The fact that Nahum's purpose is very restricted the announcement of Nineveh's destruction-accounts for his lack of reference to the sins of his own people or for the absence of any discussion of high ethical or religious themes such as is found in others of the prophets. 

Assyria! Thy sceptre lies in dust, Thy bow is broken and thy pomp has fled. 

Perished thy fruits of conquest, blood and lust, With all the warriors that Tiglath led!27 

27Pres. Charles W. Penrose.

CHAPTER IX HABAKKUK-THE PROPHET OF LIVING FAITH 

HE NAME AND PERSON OF THE PROPHET.—The name of the prophet is commonly supposed to be derived from a Hebrew root which would give it the meaning of "embrace" or "ardent embrace." There is a possibility, however, that the name is connected with the Assyrian hambakuku, the name of a garden plant.

It is unfortunate that neither history nor tradition has left us with any reliable facts concerning Habakkuk's person or the age in which he lived. Ancient Jewish rabbis suggested that the prophet was to be identified as the son of the Shunammite woman whom Elisha raised from the dead.¹ Another tradition states that Habakkuk brought food in a miraculous way to Daniel in the lion's den; and in the Preface to the same story, in the Codex Chisianus of the Septuagint, he is said to be the son of Jesus of the tribe of Levi. According to the ancient Lives of the 

Prophets he belonged to the tribe of Simeon. When

Nebuchadnezzar was advancing on Jerusalem Habakkuk is said to have fled to Ostrakine on the Egyptian coast, but returned to Palestine where he died two years before the return of the Jews under Cyrus in 537 B. C. Eusebius, the ancient church historian, states that in his day the tomb of the prophet was shown in both Gibeah and Keilah! Such are the nature of the legends and traditions that have arisen concerning Habakkuk.

As we shall see later, the prophet was a philosopher, original and forceful, inquiring and speculative, and very 

1II Kgs. 4:32-37.

sensitive to spiritual impressions. He is known as the "suppliant" among the prophets and, as the Hebrew text of the superscription implies, was also a chozeh or seer. 

THE CONTENT AND LITERARY STYLE OF THE 

PROPHECY.-The book differs markedly from the other prophetic books. Whereas most of the latter contain the words of the Lord addressed to the people, in the Book of Habakkuk the prophet, as the representative of the people, addresses and challenges the Lord. He begins by complaining about the apparent indifference of the Lord to violence, strife, and widespread corruption in Judah. 

The prophet is puzzled over this indifference, knowing as he does the righteous and holy character of God. The 

Lord in answer to this complaint states that He is about to raise up the Chaldeans to execute judgment. The prophet is only the more perplexed at this, for he fails. to understand why the Lord should use the cruel and fierce Chaldeans to execute judgment upon people who are more righteous than they are. The Lord, however, points out that the Chaldeans are to be but temporarily triumphant; they shall eventually meet with destruction, while the righteous shall live by faith. The oppressed nations may begin at once to rejoice over the fall of the 

Chaldeans; hence the prophet's "taunt-song" against them, which takes the form of five woes upon the corrupt traits in the enemy's character and his many cruelties. The book ends in a beautiful anthem of praise, called in the title of chapter three, "A prayer of Habakkuk the prophet."

The prophecy contains fifty-six verses divided between three chapters. It may be conveniently studied according to the following scheme:

. Much of what was said about the superscription in Nahum applies here.

I. 

The two-fold perplexity of Habakkuk and the Lord's solutions (1:2-2:4). 

A. Query: Why does the Lord remain apparently indifferent in the present of iniquity and violence (1:2-4)? 

B. The Lord's Answer: He is not indifferent; the 

Chaldeans are to execute His judgment (1:5-11). 

C. Query: Why does the Lord, who is pure, holy and everlasting, choose a wicked nation to punish a people more righteous than itself (1:12-17)?

D. The Lord's Answer: The Chaldeans may be tem porarily puffed up, but their end is certain; the righteous may be afflicted temporarily, but they shall live by faith forever (2:1-4). 

II. A Taunt-song over the destruction of the Chaldeans (2:5-20). 

A. Woe upon lust for conquest and spoils (2:5-8).

B. Woe upon base avarice, through which a dynasty seeks to firmly establish its rule (2:9-11).

C. Woe upon building cities with the blood and sweat of subjugated nations (2:12-14).

D. Woe upon cruelty in the treatment of conquered nations (2:15-17). 

E. Woe upon idolatry (2:18-20).

III. Habakkuk's 

(3:1-19). prayer: Petition, Appreciation, Trust

A. Superscription (3:1). 

B. Petition (3:2). 

C. A theophany (3:3-15). 

D. Calm and joyous confidence in the Lord (3:16-19).

From the standpoint of literary style the Book of Habakkuk is one of the most distinguished among the prophets. In dignity and sublimity it is surpassed by none. The prophet is distinctly original in the way he writes. Whatever he may have in common with earlier

writers is worked up in his own peculiar manner. His parallelisms are generally regular and complete. His descriptions are graphic and pointed; his expressions bold and lively. The third chapter of Habakkuk, a lyric ode or psalm, is one of the most splendid and magnificent pieces of poetry in the Old Testament. The master pen of Robert Lowth, a great English churchman, has given the following description of this sublime ode:

The prophet, indeed, illustrates this subject throughout with equal magnificence; selecting from such an assemblage of miraculous incidents, the most noble and important, displaying them in the most splendid colors, and embellishing them with the sublimest imagery, figures, and diction, the dignity of which is so heightened and recommended by the superior elegance of the conclusion, that were it not for a few shades, which the hand of time has apparently cast over it in two or three passages, no composition of the kind would, I believe, appear more elegant or more perfect than this poem.2 

Professor Franz Delitzsch, a great German scholar of the past century, spoke of Habakkuk in the following terms: 

His language is classical throughout, full of rare and select words and turns, which are to some extent exclusively his own, whilst his view and mode of presentation bear the seal of independent force and finished beauty. Notwithstanding the violent rush and lofty soaring of the thoughts, his prophecy forms a finely organized and artistically rounded whole. Like 

Isaiah, he is, comparatively speaking, much more independent of his predecessors, both in contents and form, than any other of the prophets.3

The Hebrew text of Habakkuk has not been so very 2As quoted in E. Henderson, The Book of the Twelve Minor Prophets, 2nd Ed., p. 311. 

3Quoted in C. F. Keil and F. Delitzsch, Biblical Commentary on the Old Testament (The Twelve Minor Prophets), II, 53.

well preserved and for that reason difficulties occur in translating and interpreting certain parts of the book. 

More than half of the text of Habakkuk, including the entire chapter 3, is generally rejected as not being the prophet's. The author of this work does not accede to such an action in view of the lack of positive historical proof. It is obvious to any competent authority that we do not have the text of Habakkuk exactly as it left the pen of the prophet, but it is far safer in the present state of our knowledge to assume that what has come down to us is from him than to rely on unsupported theories as to what is or what is not his. Many persons may agree with this judgment when they learn that practically every verse of the prophecy has been denied to Habakkuk by one or more scholars and that wide divergence of opinion still exists. 

THE INTERPRETATION OF HABAKKUK THE PROPHET'S FIRST QUERY.-The date of Habakkuk is so bound up with the interpretation of chapters one and two that we may consider these at once. In chapter 1:2-4 the prophet complains to the Lord about violence, iniquity, strife, and contention that cause the law to be paralyzed and judgment thwarted. The wicked also beset the righteous. 

How long, O Lord, shall I cry, And Thou wilt not hear? cry out unto Thee of violence, And Thou wilt not save. 

Why dost Thou show me iniquity, And beholdest mischief? 

And why are spoiling and violence before me?

So that there is strife, and contention ariseth.

Therefore the law is slacked, And right doth never go forth; 

For the wicked doth beset the righteous; 

Therefore right goeth forth perverted.*

The question naturally arises, Who is responsible for the wickedness, oppression, and violence? Are the 

Jews? Is the prophet giving a description of the sins of his own people? or, as critics have variously suggested, is the Jewish nation being persecuted by the Assyrians, Chaldeans, or Egyptians? The date for the writing of

Habakkuk will depend on the answers given to these questions. The author dates the prophecy about 605 

B. C. 

Unfortunately, those who hold that the Chaldeans are the oppressors and whose doom is impending, have to regard chapter 1:5-11 as an originally independent prophecy, which may have once occupied a place before chapter 1:2. By removing these verses out of their place, plausibility is given to the interpretation. Those who hold that either the Assyrians or the Egyptians are the oppressors, whose overthrow is to be accomplished by the Chaldeans, have to place chapter 1:5-11, immediately after chapter 2:4, in order to sustain their interpretation.

According to the first group of critics, the woes in chapter 

2:5-20 would be upon the Chaldeans while the second group would apply them to either the Assyrians or 

Egyptians. The Book of Habakkuk is variously dated, therefore, between 630 B. C. and 600 B. C. We shall not enter here into a more detailed discussion of the criticism of Habakkuk. 

The writer, in common with many others, believes it is possible to give a reasonable interpretation to Habakkuk without meddling with the text. I shall, therefore, 4Hab. 1:2-4.

follow the scheme of study given above. In regard to chapter 1:2-4, quoted above, the writer believes it quite natural to assume that Habakkuk has reference to the wickedness, oppression, and violence among his own people, the Jews. The prophet, as we have pointed out before, is perplexed over the apparent indifference of the Lord to the trying situation. It is quite natural for the prophet so to be. He was like many folk in our own day who wonder why the Lord seems apparently aloof to the terrible world war that is causing the death of millions and misery to countless others, many of whom can be assumed to be righteous. Habakkuk would be a good book for such folk to read. For the Lord in answer to the prophet's query as to His indifference gives him to understand that He is not indifferent. Judgment is to be executed upon the wicked, for the fierce and terrible Chaldeans are to be His agents. 

Look ye among the nations, and behold, And wonder marvelously; 

For, behold, a work shall be wrought in your days, Which ye will not believe though it be told you.

For, lo, I raise up the Chaldeans, That bitter and impetuous nation, That march through the breadth of the earth, To possess dwelling-places that are not theirs. 

They are terrible and dreadful;

Their law and their majesty proceed from themselves. 

Their horses also are swifter than leopards, And are more fierce than the wolves of the desert; 

And their horsemen spread themselves; 

Yea, their horsemen come from far, They fly as a vulture that hasteth to devour. 

They come all of them for violence;

Their faces are set eagerly as the east wind; 

And they gather captives as the sand.

And they scoff at kings, And princes are a derision unto them;

They deride every stronghold, For they heap up earth, and take it.

Then their spirit doth pass over and transgress, And they become guilty:

Even they who impute their might unto their god." 

THE PROPHET'S SECOND QUERY.-The above an swer only intensifies the prophet's complexity. How is it possible for the Lord to employ the Chaldeans and condone their cruelties? Is a wicked nation to be allowed to swallow up another nation that is more righteous than itself? Are the righteous Jews to be exterminated by the treacherous foreigners? Are the Chaldeans to have a continual triumph? This all presents a new problem to the prophet and tests his faith to the extreme. 

Art not Thou from everlasting, O Lord my God, my Holy One? 

We shall not die. 

O Lord, Thou hast ordained them for judgment, And Thou, O Rock, hast established them for correction. 

Thou that art of eyes too pure to behold evil, And that canst not look on mischief, Wherefore lookest Thou, when they deal treacherously, And beholdest Thy peace, when the wicked swalloweth up

The man that is more righteous than he; 

And makest men as the fishes of the sea, As the creeping things, that have no ruler over them?

They take up all of them with the angle, They catch them in their net, And gather them in their drag; 

Therefore they rejoice and exult.

Therefore they sacrifice unto their net, And offer unto their drag; 

5Hab. 1:5-11.

Because by them their portion is fat, And their food plenteous. 

Shall they therefore empty their net, And not spare to slay the nations continually?"

While the faith of the prophet is tested severely he does not allow it to be wrecked. He watches and waits to see what the Lord will tell him (2:1). And therein lies a profound lesson for us all. The Lord grants him a vision which is to be written down, for while its fulfillment may be delayed, it will certainly come to pass. 

I will stand upon my watch, And set me upon the tower, And will look out to see what He will speak by me, And what I shall answer when I am reproved. 

And the Lord answered me, and said: "Write the vision, And make it plain upon tables, That a man may read it swiftly,

For the vision is yet for the appointed time, And it declareth of the end, and doth not lie; 

Though it tarry, wait for it; 

Because it will surely come, it will not delay.

Behold, his soul is puffed up, it is not upright in him; 

But the righteous shall live by his faith."

The verse, "Behold his soul is puffed up, it is not upright in him; but the righteous shall live by his faith," while somewhat obscure, is one of the great passages of the Old Testament. It means essentially this: There is a moral and spiritual distinction between the Chaldeans and the people of Judah. The Chaldeans puffed up and arrogant, priding themselves in their wealth and power, and deceptive in their dealings with other nations, do not possess the moral and spiritual elements which alone can 

6Hab. 1:12-17. "Hab. 2:1-4.

insure permanence and stability. The people of the Lord, on the other hand, possess moral integrity, fidelity, and spiritual insight which insure for them a future. "The future belongs to the righteous." When the prophet says that "the righteous shall live by his faith (more accurately faithfulness)" he implies permanency.

The thought expressed by the prophet is not identical with that expressed by the apostle who quoted the words (Gal. 3:11); nevertheless the former also gives expression to a truth of profound significance. "Faithfulness" is with the prophet an external thing; it signifies integrity, fidelity, steadfastness under all provocations; but this implies, in a real sense, the New Testament conception of faith as an active principle of right conduct. 

A living faith determines conduct; religion and ethics go hand in hand, and especially in the hour of adversity a belief in Jehovah and unflinching reliance upon him are the strongest preservers of fidelity and integrity. Faith without works is dead; faith expresses itself in life. Habakkuk places chief emphasis upon the expressions of faith, and he does so rightly; but in doing this he also calls attention, by implication at least, to the motive power behind the external manifestations.8 

The statement, "the righteous shall live by his faith," is the outstanding teaching of Habakkuk. Theologically the prophet made therein an original and significant contribution to the world. Luther, Augustine, nor even Paul is to be credited with it-it belongs to Habakkuk.

The prophet is thus assured by the Lord that the righteous among His people will be in the end triumph, while the cruel and unrighteous Chaldeans must inevitably perish.

A TAUNT-SONG OVER THE CHALDEANS' DESTRUC- 

TION. Since the prophet sees that God has determined 

8F. C. Eiselen, Prophecy and the Prophets, pp. 180f.

the destruction of the cruel Chaldeans, he has the vanquished and enslaved nations lift up their voices in a series of five woes or curses in condemnation of the foe. 

We shall not quote these complete (2:5-20), but reproduce herewith the kernel or gist of them. In our age of power politics all nations seeking unrighteous dominion over others ought to consider carefully the whole tauntsong of Habakkuk. The reader is advised to study it at his earliest convenience. It has particular value in our times.

Woe unto him that increaseth that which is not his! 

Woe to him that gaineth evil gains for his house, That he may set his nest on high,

That he may be delivered from the power of evil!⁹ 

Woe to him that buildeth a town with blood, And establisheth a city by iniquity! 

Woe unto him that giveth his neighbor drink, That puttest thy venom thereto and makest him drunken also, That thou mayest look on their nakedness!10 

Woe unto him that saith to the wood: "Awake," 

To the dumb stone: "Arise"!11

HABAKKUK'S PRAYER.-This sublime ode, prayer, psalm or rhapsody, as it has been called, is now generally denied to the prophet. I frankly see no need to do this. 

9"As the eagle builds its nest on high, to protect it from harm, so does the Chaldean seek to elevate and strengthen his rule by robbery and plunder, that it may never be wrested from his family again." 

10A figurative representation of the seducing arts by which the Chaldeans overthrow their victims. The nakedness is a figure of the shame that falls on the victim. How appropriate these statements are to describe the methods of a Hitler! 

11 An obvious curse upon the idolatrous nation.

Supposing the style and temper of the poem is a little different from the first two chapters, can a prophet not have a change of mood? Certainly the early part of

Habakkuk was great poetry and the last chapter corresponds. And the reasons given by the later critics for denying the poem to Habakkuk are too subjective to be convincing.

The superscription of the prayer reminds one of certain titles in the Psalms. 

A prayer of Habakkuk the prophet. 

Upon Shigionoth.12 

The "shigionoth" are supposed to be stringed instruments. Possibly the prayer was set to music anciently and used in the Temple.

The prophet, first of all, petitions the Lord to renew 

His loving kindness, power, and goodness in delivering

His people as He did in days of old. In other words, he wants a revival of God's work for the benefit of his people now living "in the midst of the years." 

O Lord, I have heard the report of Thee, and am afraid; 

O Lord, revive Thy work in the midst of the years, In the midst of the years make it known; 

In wrath remember compassion.13

In verses 3-15 we have a magnificent theophany in which the prophet describes the coming of the Lord in the glory of His omnipotence, to deliver His people and His anointed from the bondage of the power of the world. 

Pestilence and great earthly manifestations precede His indignant march to thresh the nations in anger. 

God cometh from Teman, 12Hab. 3:1. 

13Hab. 3:2.

And the Holy One from mount Paran. Selah 

His glory covereth the heavens, And the earth is full of His praise. 

And a brightness appeareth as the light;

Rays hath He at His side; 

And there is the hiding of His power. 

Before Him goeth the pestilence, And fiery bolts go forth at His feet. 

He standeth, and shaketh the earth,

He beholdeth, and maketh the nations to tremble; 

And the everlasting mountains are dashed in pieces, The ancient hills do bow; 

His goings are as of old. 

I see the tents of Cushan in affliction;

The curtains of the Lord of Midian do tremble. 

Is it, O Lord, that against the rivers, Is it that Thine anger is kindled against the rivers, Or Thy wrath against the sea? 

That Thou dost ride upon Thy horses, Upon Thy chariots of victory? 

Thy bow is made quite bare; 

Sworn are the rods of the word.

Selah. 

Thou dost cleave the earth with rivers.

The mountains have seen Thee, and they tremble; 

The tempest of waters floweth over; 

The deep uttereth its voice, And lifteth up its hands on high. 

The sun and moon stand still in their habitation; 

At the light of Thine arrows as they go, At the shining of Thy glittering spear. 

Thou marchest through the earth in indignation, Thou threshest the nations in anger. 

Thou art come forth for the deliverance of Thy people,

For the deliverance of Thine anointed; 

Thou woundest the head out of the house of the wicked, Uncovering the foundation even unto the neck. Selah. 

Thou hast stricken through with his own rods the head of his rulers,

That come as a whirlwind to scatter me;

Whose rejoicing is as to devour the poor secretly. 

Thou has trodden the sea with Thy horses, The foaming of mighty waters.14

Finally, the prophet ends his prayer with an expression of calm and joyous confidence in the Lord (3:16-19). As an expression of living faith verses 17-19 are unsurpassed in the Old Testament. 

When I heard, mine inward parts trembled, My lips quivered at the voice; 

Rottenness entereth into my bones, And I tremble where I stand; 

That I should wait for the day of trouble,

When he cometh up against the people that he invadeth. 

For though the fig-tree shall not blossom, Neither shall fruit be in the vines; 

The labor of the olive shall fail, And the fields shall yield no food;

The flock shall be cut off from the fold, And there shall be no herd in the stalls; 

Yet I will rejoice in the Lord, I will exult in the God of my salvation. 

God, the Lord, is my strength, And He maketh my feet like hinds' feet, And He maketh me to walk upon my high places. 

For the Leader. With my string-music.15

To the other great lesson we have already learned in Habakkuk, namely, that "the righteous shall live by his faith," we must add another. It is that the Lord is

supreme over all the earth. The destinies of all nations lie in His hands. The wicked may temporarily triumph, but their rule is eventually cut short for righteousness must achieve supremacy in a world over which the Lord is head.

CHAPTER X ZEPHANIAH-PROPHET OF THE LORD'S DAY OF WRATH 

'HE PROPHET'S NAME AND GENEALOGY.-Zeph aniah's name means "Jehovah hides" or "Jehovah has hidden." He is the only one of the prophets of whom a considerable genealogy is given in a superscription. This I consider due probably to the fact that he was a descendant of Hezekiah, king of Judah. According to the record, he was a great-greatgrandson of the Hebrew king. 

The word of the Lord which came unto Zephaniah the son of Cushi, the son of Gedaliah, the son of Amariah, the son of Hezekiah, in the days of Josiah the son of Amon, king of Judah.¹

Zephaniah very probably lived in Jerusalem, judging from his familiar reference to parts of the city, such as the "fish gate" and "Maktesh," the merchants' quarter.2 

Also his reference to "this place" in 1:4 strongly supports that opinion.

Two other Zephaniah's are mentioned in the Old Testament besides the prophet. Little is known of Zephaniah's personal history aside from what has been indicated or the few facts that may be gathered from his utterances. There is an ancient tradition that he was of the tribe of Simeon, but this is considered worthless by most authorities.

THE PROPHET'S TIMES.-Since Zephaniah's proph- 1Zeph. I:I. 

2Zeph. 1:10, 1I. 

See I Chron. 6:36; II Kgs. 25:18; Jer. 21:1; 37:3.

ecy is dated in the days of Josiah, king of Judah, his ministry lasted until at least the year 639 B. C., the time of the king's accession, or stretched well into the years of the monarch's reign of thirty-one years. Times must have been turbulent in Judah when Josiah ascended the throne. The Book of II Kings relates that Josiah's father, 

Amon, was slain in his own house by conspiring servants, and that the people of the land in turn slew them and secured the throne for Josiah, then a child of eight. We are also informed that Amon's reign of two years was evil like that of his father Manasseh before him. Idolatry, therefore, went on apace and also the shedding of blood.* 

Since Josiah was a good king, it would appear that a strong group of men and women with character took charge of the king in his early years of the throne and thus paved the way for the reforms that took place in his lifetime. But the evil years of the reigns of Manasseh and Amon must have continued to affect the worship and conduct of the Hebrew people adversely during the early years of Josiah's reign. Zephaniah mentions the remnant of Baal still in the land, the kemarim or idolpriests who probably went about in black, the people who worshipped the host of heaven upon the housetops, and those who swear by Jehovah and Malkam. Many people had apostatized from or had grown indifferent to the true worship of Jehovah." The nobility affected foreign fashions and clothing in direct violation of the Law of Moses, which provided for clothing that was designed to preserve the Hebrew people distinct from others. The

rich of the day were rapacious and cruel. Even the servants filled their masters' houses "with violence and deceit."9

Social injustice and corruption were rampant, 10 professional prophets were wanton and treacherous, and priests violated or outraged the law when expounding it to their people.11 Jeremiah, who was probably a contemporary of Zephaniah, mentions also the worship of the 

Queen of Heaven. 12 Zephaniah may have lived to see and take part in the religious reforms of Josiah, which commenced after the finding of "the book of the law" in the Temple of Hilkiah the priest. 13 The account of these reforms mentioned in II Kings 23 indicates that the times of Zephaniah before 621 B. C. were even worse than we have described. Consequently we can understand and appreciate the vehemence and power with which the prophet denounced Judah. The people, however, seem to have been "settled on their lees,"14 i. e., they were corrupt, selfish, and indifferent to such an extent that the message of Zephaniah failed to influence them. They said in their hearts, "The Lord will not do good, neither will he do evil."15 They were, therefore, ready for the promised destruction.

THE DATE, CONTENTS, AND LITERARY CHARAC- TERISTICS OF THE BOOK.-The exact date of the Book of Zephaniah is not known. Those who attempt to date it usually do so with reference to Josiah's reforms which 

9Zeph. 1:9. 

10Zeph. 3:1, 3, 7. 

11Zeph. 3:4. 

12 See Jer. 7:18; 44:19. 

13 See the full account in II Kgs. 22, 23. 

14Zeph. 1:12. The metaphor has reference to the sediment that settles at the bottom of a cask of wine. 

15Zeph. 1:12.

began in 621 B. C. There are some scholars who would place the book after that time, but the general concensus seems to favor a date a few years earlier, let us say about 

626 B. C.

The content of the book centers about the great theme, the coming of the Day of the Lord. It will be remembered how much the same theme figures in the prophecies of Joel and Obadiah. The book divides quite naturally into two parts of unequal length. The first part, 1:2-3:7, deals with the near approach of the terrible day and contains almost exclusively threats and denunciations. The second, 3:8-20, contains a beautiful picture of the eventual salvation, glorification, and joy of the redeemed Israel. The book may be treated under the following heads: I.

The universality of God's judgments upon the earth (1:2-3:8). 

A. The terror of the Day of the Lord-the only way to escape is to seek the Lord. Judah especially threatened (1:2-2:3). 

B. Destruction of Philistia, Moab and Ammon, Ethiopia and Assyria, nations which had always been hostile to Israel (2:4-15).

C. A special denunciation of Jerusalem, the polluted city (3:1-7). 

D. A key verse (3:8). 

II. A vision of the latter days (3:9-20). Salvation and blessing to come upon the repentant, especially the "remnant of Israel," who will be praised among the peoples of the earth as the redeemed of the Lord. 

The Book of Zephaniah, as you may have noticed, contains three short chapters. The style of the prophet reminds one somewhat of Isaiah, though his poetry is not as good. His message demanded vigor and clarity; these

he supplied with a sure hand, thus in part making up for lack of elegance and charm. Large portions of chapters 2 and 3 are written in a vivid dirge-like meter, i. e., in lines of 3 plus 2 beats each. Chapter one can be classed decidedly as oratory. Repetition of words and phrases helps to enhance the emphasis desired by the prophet. 

Occasional puns and epigrams tend to increase the reader's interest, especially if he can use the Hebrew text. 

The originality of practically every verse in chapters 2 and 3 has been questioned at some time or other by one or more authorities. Nor has chapter 1 entirely escaped. There may be some minor interpolations, but the author feels that the text as a whole must have come from Zephaniah. Criticisms of a particular verse or of larger portions of the text as not being from Zephaniah are bound, in general, to be very subjective in nature and incapable of absolute proof. The Hebrew text of Zephaniah has come down to us fairly well preserved. Very often the 

Septuagint (Greek Version) helps where error is suspected. 

Zephaniah's terrible note of doom for the nations has attracted the notice of many men through the ages. 

In the thirteenth century an Italian Franciscan monk, Thomas of Celano, was so impressed by it that he gave to the world a great poem, Dies Irae, Dies Illa,16 "so awful in its grandeur and so intense in its earnestness and pathos that we hear in it, as it were, 'the final crash of the universe!'"

THE INTERPRETATION OF ZEPHANIAH'S PROPHECY. 

-Zephaniah is unquestionably one of the most difficult of the prophetic books. One who reads it for the first time, even in a modern translation, will probably come away somewhat bewildered for the experience. The book 

16"Day of wrath! O day of mourning." See Zeph. 1:15.

20 should be read several times and then pondered over. One cannot expect to catch the spirit of the prophet without considerable effort. The great theme of the book, as we have already pointed out, is the terrible Day of the Lord. 

Note the following expressions indicative of that fact: "The day of the Lord is at hand,"17 "the day of the 

Lord's sacrifice,"18 "the great day of the Lord is near,”’19 "that day is a day of wrath . . . trouble and distress wasteness and desolation . etc., "He will make an end, yea, a terrible end,”21 "the day when one passeth as the chaff,"22 "the day that I rise up to testify."23 Let the reader keep in mind that Zephaniah's fundamental concept of the Day of the Lord is essentially the same as that of Joel, Obadiah, and Malachi. They all understood well that the term referred to the Second Advent of the Lord in the latter days with its accompanying scenes of judgment and desolation. Each of them used the concept in accordance with his own needs and with reference to particular truths or teachings he wanted made known to his own or to a future generation. 

Thus, Malachi makes known that Elijah is to precede the "great and terrible day of the Lord"24 and that the Lord is to "purify the sons of Levi."25 It does not follow, because the other prophets do not mention the coming of Elijah and the sons of Levi, that they did not understand these matters as well as Malachi. Joel mentions 

17Zeph. 1:7. 

18Zeph. 1:8. 

19Zeph. 1:14. 

20Zeph. 1:15. 

21Zeph. 1:18. 22Zeph. 2:2. 

23Zeph. 3:8. 

24Mal. 4:5. 

25 Mal. 3:3.

great cosmic signs in heaven and on earth that are to be seen before the Day of the Lord comes. 26 Malachi and the other prophets do not refer to these facts, at least not so specifically as Joel, but they probably understood them just as well. As we have implied, each may emphasize some particular truth or truths, respecting the doctrine of the Day of the Lord, not appearing in the teachings of the others. Zephaniah's use of the theme of the Day of the Lord was brought about by the wickedness of the people of Judah. Their crimes and excesses merited a stern and vigorous rebuke. Knowing the great judgments and desolations that were to descend upon the world in the latter days at the Lord's Second Advent,

Zephaniah determined to bring home to his people the awful retribution that should shortly befall them in terms descriptive of that great but future event in world history. Zephaniah's prophecy is definitely apocalyptic; it is a great revelation of the future, designed to impress the people of his own and future times that crime, wickedness, and religious indifference cannot be forever condoned by a patient and merciful God. That which men sow they must inevitably reap. Such a lesson is of abiding value. For that reason the Book of Zephaniah is another prophetic warning to our own generation. In form the prophecy is a series of denunciations, threats, and warnings to Judah and the nations that were always hostile to the Hebrews, set in a framework descriptive of the great and terrible day of the Lord. The latter part of the prophecy, chapter 3:8-20, is intended to console the righteous of his (Zephaniah's) and succeeding generations in the thought that God will remember them and eventually bring to pass a just and better world order 26 Joel 2:30.

over which "the king of Israel, even Jehovah" will preside. 

With these explanations we may now examine the prophecy in some detail.

A DECLARATION OF WORLD JUDGMENT.-In omi nous and portentous language, reminding one in certain respects of the opening verses of the Book of Joel, the prophecy of Zephaniah begins by announcing a great world judgment. Exaggerated figures are purposely used to bring home to men the terrible nature of God's judgments in the great Day of the Lord. 

I will utterly sweep away all things 

From off the face of the earth, Saith the Lord. 

I will sweep away man and beast, Sweep away the fowls of the heaven, and the fishes of the sea, And the offences with the wicked; 

And I will cut off man from the face of the earth, Saith the Lord.27

Having announced the theme of world judgment, Zephaniah then proceeds to play upon it like a composer does a theme in a symphony he is creating. The prophet turns special attention to the Jews-naturally his primary interest lies in the fate of his own people. He sees the terrible arm of the Lord reaching out to punish them for their misdeeds. This punishment may be near at hand for Judah,28 but is announced in terms of world judgment far away in point of time. The reasons for punishment are given in some detail.

THE DESTRUCTION OF FOREIGN NATIONS HOS- 

TILE TO JUDAH.-The next movement-if we may call it such of Zephaniah's symphony of world judgment concerns those nations traditionally hostile to Judah. The prophet has already predicted the punishment of his own people, but he knows that the foreign nations who have taunted and reviled them are even more worthy of destruction. So he names them one by one and pronounces 

God's judgments upon them. The cities of Gaza, Ashkelon, Ashdod, and Ekron, representing the Philistines, are to be forsaken, desolated, rooted up, and destroyed. 

They shall become meadows and folds for flocks to be inherited by the remnant of Judah when the Lord turns their captivity. 

For Gaza shall be forsaken, And Ashkelon a desolation; 

They shall drive out Ashdod at the noonday, And Ekron shall be rooted up.

Woe unto the inhabitants of the sea-coast, The nation of the Cherethites! 

The word of the Lord is against you, O Canaan, the land of the Philistines;

I will even destroy thee, that there shall be no inhabitant.

And He will stretch forth His hand against the north, And destroy Assyria;

All of these nations in the course of time met the judgments decreed by God through Zephaniah. The break-up of the Assyrian Empire was completed by 610 

B. C., about sixteen years after Zephaniah's prophecy was uttered. The great city of Nineveh fell in 612 B. C., under the onslaught of the Medes and Chaldeans. This has already been discussed in the chapter on Nahum. After going over the district occupied by the Philistines a few years ago, the author would judge that little has been left of the cities of that nation. And history attests the 

41Zeph. 2:13-15.

fall of the Moabites, Ammonites, and Ethiopians. The captivity of the Jews has not as yet been completely turned so that they can inherit the territory of Moab, Ammon, and Philistia, but judging from present events in the world, it would be a rash man who would predict that such could not take place in the course of the next few years. About 450,000 Jews are now in Palestine.

JERUSALEM THE POLLUTED CITY.-The prophet now turns his attention back to his own people. He varies the theme of judgment a little by centering his attention on Jerusalem rather than denouncing Judah as a whole.

Woe to her that is rebellious and polluted, The oppressing city! 

She hearkened not to the voice, She received not correction; 

She trusted not in the Lord, She drew not near to her God. 

Her princes in the midst of her are roaring lions; 

Her judges are evening wolves, They do not strip off the bones in the morning.42 

Her prophets are wanton 

And treacherous men; 

Her priests have profaned that which is holy, They have done violence to the law.

The Lord who is righteous is in the midst of her, He will not do unrighteousness; 

Every morning doth He bring His right to light, It faileth not; 

But the unrighteous knoweth no shame. 

I have cut off nations, Their corners are desolate; 

I have laid waste their streets, 42The meaning of the original Hebrew in this line is uncertain. The writer believes that the simplest explanation is that the judges, here compared to evening wolves, do not strip off bones in the morning, i. e., in their avarice they finish their preying at night.

So that none passeth by;

Their cities are destroyed, so that there is no man, So that there is no inhabitant. 

I said: "Surely thou wilt fear Me, Thou wilt receive correction; 

So her dwelling shall not be cut off, All that I had appointed concerning her"; 

But they hastened the more to corrupt all their doings.43

This is a severe arraignment of Zephaniah's own people and is truly prophetic. Israel has special privileges, hence responsibilities inevitably follow. A chosen people elects spiritual responsibilities, and when they choose to turn away from God their doom follows. Zephaniah's warning left no room for misunderstanding, particularly on the part of Israel's leaders.

A KEY VERSE.-In the following verse, 3:18, we have a transition between the denunciations, threats, and warnings that have gone before and the ideal picture to follow. Not only so, but the verse is important as a check to our interpretation of the book. In a word, it is a key. In my interpretation of Zephaniah I have assumed that the prophet warns Judah and the other nations of near destruction in terms of the great Day of the Lord, which was centuries removed from Zephaniah's time and, in fact, is still future. In the verse under consideration that interpretation seems to be borne out. The Lord addresses Himself to the righteous, apparently of Zephaniah's own generation, and tells them of the great day in which God will pour out His indignation and fiery anger upon the kingdoms of the earth. To Latter-day Saints it is clear from the verses immediately following that this judgment is to take place in the present dispensation, since 

43Zeph. 3:1-7.

they refer to the gathering of Israel. 

Therefore, wait ye for Me, saith the Lord,

Until the day that I rise up to testify; 

For My determination is to gather nations, To assemble kingdoms, To pour out upon them Mine indignation, All the heat of My wrath;

For with the fire of my zeal 

The whole earth shall be consumed.44

A VISION OF THE LATTER DAYS.-Zephaniah, like so many of the prophets, finds it hard indeed to see only the gloomy and evil side of his people. Idealistic as he is, he looks also for the good. Not all of Israel are to be destroyed. There will still exist in the centuries to come a righteous remnant with whom God can work and bring to pass His righteous purposes in the earth. Since the prophet has warned his people in terms of the world judgment of the latter days, he feels it only just to hold out to them the rosy and beautiful picture of gathered and redeemed Israel of the same era. If it doesn't impress the wicked and cause their reform, it will at least comfort the righteous and show them that their lives and work will not have been in vain. 

For then15 will I turn to the peoples

That they may all call upon the name of the Lord, To serve Him unitedly.

The prophet then shows that the remnant of Israel will be ashamed for their doings, their iniquities, and deceitful ways. As an afflicted and poor people they shall take refuge in the Lord. Finally, the prophet bursts into a paean of song and rejoicing over the redeemed Israel, whose Lord is in the midst of her. 

Sing, O daughter of Zion, Shout, O Israel;

At that time will I bring you in, And at that time will I gather you;
